# Skills in Mathematics for JEE Main & Advanced: Play with Graphs
Sometimes the visual understanding of a mathematical problem is quite helpful in solving it fast and correctly. Play with Graphs is an attempt to ease the minds of all mathematics lovers and Engineering aspirants who wish to solve the tricky and knotty mathematics problems involving functional approach. This book helps you make intuitive connections between the graphs of the functions in the problem with the exact solution of the problem. The book starts with a discussion of the properties of various standard functions with the help of their graphs and later takes up the topics such as curvature and transformations, asymptotes, the singular points and finally the idea of curve tracing.

Recommended to all those who sincerely desire to master problem-solving in Mathematics.

## About the Author
Amit M. Agarwal is a renowned name and an authority in the field of Mathematics with a number of accolades to his credit. Immediately after doing his Post Graduation from Meerut University in 1997, Mr. Agarwal started teaching Mathematics in his own institute at Meerut and simultaneously writing the books for JEE. Amit sir's students has always been among the good JEE Ranks. For the last 20 years he is continuously teaching JEE aspirants and writing & revising the books. Presently he is teaching in Mumbai.

## Preface
It is a matter of great pleasure and pride for me to introduce to you this book "Play with Graphs". As a teacher, guiding the Engineering aspirants for over a decade now, I have always been in the lookout for right approach to understand various mathematical problems. I had always felt the need of a book that can develop and sharpen the ideas of the students within a very short span of time.

The book in your hands, aims to help you solve various mathematical problems by the use of graphs. Ways to draw different types of graphs are very easy and can be understood by even an average student. I feel glad to mention that the use of graphs in solving various mathematical problems has been well illustrated in this book.

I would like to take this opportunity to thank M/s Arihant Prakashan for assigning this work to me. It is their inspiration that has encouraged me to bring this book in this present form.

I would also like to thank Arihant DTP Unit for the nice laser typesetting.

Valuable suggestions from the students and teachers are always welcome, and these will find due places in the ensuing editions.

— Amit M. Agarwal

In this section, we shall revise some basic curves which are given as.

## 1.1 Algebraic Functions

### 1. Polynomial Function

A function of the form:

$f(x) = a_0 + a_1x + a_2x^2 + \dots + a_nx^n;$

where $n \in \mathbb{N}$ and $a_0, a_1, a_2, \dots, a_n \in \mathbb{R}$.

Then, $f$ is called a polynomial function. "$f(x)$ is also called polynomial in $x$".

--- Page 9 ---

## Some Basic Polynomial Functions

### (i) Identity Function/Graph of $f(x) = x$

A function $f$ defined by $f(x) = x$ for all $x \in R$, is called the identity function.

Here, $y = x$ clearly represents a straight line passing through the origin and inclined at an angle of $45^\circ$ with $x$-axis shown as: 
[Figure of a straight line with a slope of 1 passing through the origin, making an angle of $45^\circ$ with the $x$-axis].

The domain and range of identity functions are both equal to $R$.

### (ii) Graph of $f(x) = x^2$

A function given by $f(x) = x^2$ is called the square function.

The domain of square function is $R$ and its range is $R^+ \cup \{0\}$ or $[0, \infty)$.

Clearly $y = x^2$, is a parabola. Since $y = x^2$ is an even function, so its graph is symmetrical about $y$-axis, shown as:
[Figure of a parabola opening upwards with vertex at the origin, symmetrical about the $y$-axis].

### (iii) Graph of $f(x) = x^3$

A function given by $f(x) = x^3$ is called the cube function.

The domain and range of cube are both equal to $R$.

Since, $y = x^3$ is an odd function, so its graph is symmetrical about opposite quadrant, i.e., "origin", shown as:
[Figure of a cubic curve passing through the origin, symmetrical about the origin].

### (iv) Graph of $f(x) = x^{2n}; n \in N$

If $n \in N$, then function $f$ given by $f(x) = x^{2n}$ is an even function.

So, its graph is always symmetrical about $y$-axis.

Also, $x^2 > x^4 > x^6 > x^8 > \cdots$ for all $x \in (0, 1)$

and $x^2 < x^4 < x^6 < x^8 < \cdots$ for all $x \in (1, \infty)$.

Graphs of $y = x^2, y = x^4, y = x^6, \ldots$, etc. are shown as:
[Figure of multiple parabolas opening upwards with vertices at the origin, symmetrical about the $y$-axis, with decreasing width as the exponent increases].

### (v) Graph of $f(x) = x^{2n-1}; n \in N$

If $n \in N$, then the function $f$ given by $f(x) = x^{2n-1}$ is an odd function.

So, its graph is symmetrical about origin or opposite quadrants.

[No specific figure description provided, but it can be inferred that it would be similar to Fig. 1.3].

--- Page 10 ---

## Rational Expression

A function obtained by dividing a polynomial by another polynomial is called a rational function.

$$
f(x) = \frac{P(x)}{Q(x)}
$$

Domain $R$ except those points for which denominator $= 0$.

### Graphs of some Simple Rational Functions

#### (i) Graph of $f(x) = \frac{1}{x}$

A function defined by $f(x) = \frac{1}{x}$ is called the reciprocal function or rectangular hyperbola, with coordinate axis as asymptotes. The domain and range of $f(x) = \frac{1}{x}$ is $R - \{0\}$.

Since, $f(x)$ is odd function, so its graph is **symmetrical about opposite quadrants**. Also, we observe

$$
\lim_{x \to 0^+} f(x) = +\infty \quad \text{and} \quad \lim_{x \to 0^-} f(x) = -\infty
$$

Thus, $f(x) = \frac{1}{x}$ could be shown as in [a graph of a rectangular hyperbola with asymptotes at $x=0$ and $y=0$, passing through $(1,1)$].

#### (ii) Graph of $f(x) = \frac{1}{x^2}$

Here, $f(x) = \frac{1}{x^2}$ is an even function, so its graph is **symmetrical about $y$-axis**.

Domain of $f(x)$ is $R - \{0\}$ and range is $(0, \infty)$.

Also, as $y \to \infty$ as $\lim_{x \to 0^+} f(x)$ or $\lim_{x \to 0^-} f(x)$

and $y \to 0$ as $\lim_{x \to \pm \infty} f(x)$.

Thus, $f(x) = \frac{1}{x^2}$ could be shown as in [a graph of a parabola-like curve with a vertical asymptote at $x=0$, approaching $(0, \infty)$ as $x$ approaches $0$ and approaching $0$ as $x$ approaches $\pm \infty$].

--- Page 11 ---

### Cleaned Textbook Page in Markdown

## 3. Irrational Function

The algebraic function containing terms having non-integral rational powers of $x$ are called irrational functions.

### Graphs of Some Simple Irrational Functions

#### (i) Graph of $f(x) = x^{1/2}$

Here; $f(x) = \sqrt{x}$ is the portion of the parabola $y^2 = x$, which lies above $x$-axis.

Domain of $f(x)$ $\mathbb{R}^+ \backslash \{0\}$ or $[0, \infty)$  
and range of $f(x)$ $\mathbb{R}^+ \backslash \{0\}$ or $[0, \infty)$.

Thus, the graph of $f(x) = x^{1/2}$ is shown as;  
[The graph of $y = \sqrt{x}$, a curve starting from the origin $(0,0)$ and increasing to the right, passing through $(1,1)$]

### Note

If $f(x) = x^n$ and $g(x) = x^{1/n}$, then $f(x)$ and $g(x)$ are inverse of each other.  
$f(x) = x^n$ and $g(x) = x^{1/n}$ is the mirror image about $y = x$.

## Previous Sections

### (iii) Graph of $f(x) = \frac{1}{x^{2n-1}}; n \in N$

Here, $f(x) = \frac{1}{x^{2n-1}}$ is an odd function, so its graph is symmetrical in opposite quadrants.  
Also, $y \to \infty$ when $\lim_{x \to 0^+} f(x)$ and  
$y \to -\infty$ when $\lim_{x \to 0^-} f(x)$.  

Thus, the graph for $f(x) = \frac{1}{x^3}$; $f(x) = \frac{1}{x^5}$, etc. will be similar to the graph of $f(x) = \frac{1}{x}$ which has asymptotes as coordinate axes, shown as in [Fig. 1.8: A graph with two asymptotes, $x=0$ and $y=0$, and two curves in the first and third quadrants].

### (iv) Graph of $f(x) = \frac{1}{x^{2n}}; n \in N$

We observe that the function $f(x) = \frac{1}{x^{2n}}$ is an even function, so its graph is symmetrical about $y$-axis.  
Also, $y \to \infty$ as $\lim_{x \to 0^+} f(x)$ or $\lim_{x \to 0^-} f(x)$  
and $y \to 0$ as $\lim_{x \to \infty} f(x)$ or $\lim_{x \to -\infty} f(x)$.  

The values of $y$ decrease as the values of $x$ increase.  
Thus, the graph of $f(x) = \frac{1}{x^2}$; $f(x) = \frac{1}{x^4}$, etc. will be similar as the graph of $f(x) = \frac{1}{x^2}$, which has asymptotes as coordinate axis. Shown as in [Fig. 1.9: A graph with two asymptotes, $x=0$ and $y=0$, and two curves in the first and second quadrants].

## Introduction to Graphs

### Graph of $f(x) = x^{1/3}$

As discussed above, if $g(x) = x^3$. Then $f(x) = x^{1/3}$ is image of $g(x)$ about $y = x$.

where domain of $f(x)$ $\mathbb{R}$.

and range of $f(x)$ $\mathbb{R}$.

Thus, the graph of $f(x) = x^{1/3}$ is shown in [a graph of $y=x^{1/3}$, symmetric about $y=x$, passing through the origin].

### Graph of $f(x) = x^{1/2n}$; $n \in \mathbb{N}$

Here, $f(x) = x^{1/2n}$ is defined for all $x \in [0, \infty)$ and the values taken by $f(x)$ are positive.

So, domain and range of $f(x)$ are $[0, \infty)$.

Here, the graph of $f(x) = x^{1/2n}$ is the mirror image of the graph of $f(x) = x^{2n}$ about the line $y = x$, when $x \in [0, \infty)$.

Thus, $f(x) = x^{1/2}$, $f(x) = x^{1/4}$, $\ldots$ are shown as;

### Graph of $f(x) = x^{1/2n - 1}$, when $n \in \mathbb{N}$

Here, $f(x) = x^{1/2n - 1}$ is defined for all $x \in \mathbb{R}$, and range of $f(x)$ $\mathbb{R}$. So, graph of $f(x) = x^{1/2n - 1}$ is the mirror image of the graph of $f(x) = x^{2n - 1}$ about the line $y = x$ when $x \in \mathbb{R}$.

Thus, $f(x) = x^{1/3}$, $f(x) = x^{1/5}$, $\ldots$ are shown as;

[Graphs of $y=x^{1/2}$, $y=x^{1/4}$, $y=x^{1/3}$, $y=x^{1/5}$, and $y=x$ for comparison]

## Note

We have discussed some of the simple curves for Polynomial, Rational and Irrational functions. Graphs of the some more difficult rational functions will be discussed in chapter 3. Such as;

$$
y = \frac{x}{x + 1}
$$

$$
y = \frac{1}{x^2 - 1}
$$

$$
y = \frac{x^2 + x + 1}{x^2 - x + 1}
$$

## 4. Piecewise Functions

As discussed piecewise functions are:

*   (a) Absolute value function (or modulus function)
*   (b) Signum function.
*   (c) Greatest integer function.
*   (d) Fractional part function.
*   (e) Least integer function.

### (a) Absolute value function (or modulus function)

$$
y = |x| = 
\begin{cases}
x, & x \geq 0 \\
-x, & x < 0
\end{cases}
$$

--- Page 13 ---

## Properties of Modulus Functions

The modulus function is defined as:

*   It is the numerical value of $x$.
*   It is symmetric about $y$-axis with a domain of $\mathbb{R}$ and a range of $[0, \infty)$.

### Properties

*   (i) $|x| \iff a \iff x = \pm a; (a \geq 0)$
*   (ii) $|x| \geq a \iff x \leq -a$ or $x \geq a; (a \geq 0)$
*   (iii) $|x + y| \leq |x| + |y|$
*   (iv) $|x + y| \geq | |x| - |y| |$

## Signum Function

The signum function is defined as:

$y = \text{Sgn}(x) = \begin{cases} \frac{|x|}{x} \text{ or } \frac{x}{|x|} & x \neq 0 \\ +1, & \text{if } x > 0 \\ 0, & \text{if } x = 0 \\ -1, & \text{if } x < 0 \end{cases}$

*   Domain of $f(x)$: $\mathbb{R}$
*   Range of $f(x)$: $\{-1, 0, 1\}$

[Graph of $y = |x|$: A V-shaped graph with its vertex at the origin, opening upwards, symmetric about the $y$-axis.]

[Graph of $y = \text{Sgn}(x)$: A step function with three horizontal line segments at $y = -1$, $y = 0$, and $y = 1$, with a jump at $x = 0$.]

## Greatest Integer Function

$[x]$ indicates the integral part of $x$ which is nearest and smaller integer to $x$. It is also known as the **floor of $x$**.

Thus, $[2.3] = 2$, $[0.2] = 0$, $[2] = 2$, $[8.0725] = 8$.

In general: $n \iff x < n + 1$ $(n \in \mathbb{Z}) \iff [x] = n$.

### Properties

*   (i) $[x] = x$, if $x$ is an integer.
*   (ii) $[x + I] = [x] + I$, if $I$ is an integer.
*   (iii) $[x + y] \geq [x] + [y]$.
*   (iv) If $[f(x)] \geq I$, then $f(x) \geq I$.
*   (v) If $[f(x)] \iff I$, then $f(x) < I + 1$.
*   (vi) $[ -x ] = - [x]$, if $x$ is an integer.
*   (vii) $[ -x ] = - [x] - 1$, if $x$ is not an integer.

"It is also known as a **stepwise function/floor of $x$**."

[Graph of $y = [x]$: A step function with a series of horizontal line segments, with each step starting at an integer value on the $x$-axis and having a height equal to that integer value.]

--- Page 14 ---

### Fractional Part of Function

The fractional part of $x$ is denoted by $\{x\}$. We can express $x$ as the sum of its integer part $[x]$ and its fractional part $\{x\}$, such that $x = [x] + \{x\}$, where $0'' < \{x\} < 1''$. 

The function $y = \{x\}$ can be represented graphically as [a graph with a sawtooth pattern, where the graph increases linearly from 0 to 1 as $x$ increases from $n$ to $n+1$ for any integer $n$].

Properties of fractional part of $x$:
(i) $\{x\} = x$ if $0'' < x < 1''$.
(ii) $\{x\} = 0$ if $x$ is an integer.
(iii) $\{x + n\} = \{x\}$ if $n$ is an integer.

### Least Integer Function

The least integer function, denoted by $(x)$ or $\lceil x \rceil$, is defined as the smallest integer greater than or equal to $x$. It is also known as the ceiling of $x$. 

Thus, $\lceil 2.3023 \rceil = 3$, $\lceil 0.23 \rceil = 1$, $\lceil 8.0725 \rceil = 9$, and $\lceil 0.6 \rceil = 1$.

In general, 
$\lceil x \rceil = n+1$ if $n < x < n+1$ ($n$ is an integer).

The function $y = \lceil x \rceil$ can be represented graphically as [a step function where the value of $y$ increases by 1 at each integer value of $x$].

Properties of least integer function:
(i) $\lceil x \rceil = x$ if $x$ is an integer.
(ii) $\lceil x + n \rceil = \lceil x \rceil + n$ if $n$ is an integer.
(iii) The greatest integer function converts $x = I + f$ to $\lceil x \rceil = I + 1$ while $x$ converts to $(\lceil x \rceil)$. 

### Note
We shall discuss the curves $y = \{\sin x\}$, $y = \{x^3\}$, $y = \{\sin^{-1} (\sin x)\}$, $y = [\sin x]$, etc. in Chapter 2 (Curvature and Transformations).

## 1. Trigonometric Function

### (a) Sine function

Here, $f(x) = \sin x$ can be discussed in two ways i.e., Graph diagram and Circle diagram where

Domain of sine function is $\mathbb{R}$ and range is $[-1, 1]$.

#### Graph diagram
[ A graph showing a sine wave with amplitude 1, period $2\pi$, and phase shift 0. The graph passes through the points $(-\frac{3\pi}{2}, -1)$, $(-\frac{\pi}{2}, -1)$, $(\frac{\pi}{2}, 1)$, and $(\frac{3\pi}{2}, -1)$. ]

On $x$-axis and $y$-axis
$f(x) = \sin x$, increases strictly from -1 to 1 as $x$ increases from $\frac{-\pi}{2}$ to $\frac{\pi}{2}$, decreases strictly from 1 to -1 as $x$ increases from $\frac{\pi}{2}$ to $\frac{3\pi}{2}$ and so on. We have graph as;

#### Circle diagram

(On trigonometric plane or using quadrants). Let a circle of radius '1', i.e., unit circle.

Then,
$\sin \theta = \frac{a}{1}$,
$\sin \theta = \frac{b}{1}$,
$\sin \theta = \frac{c}{-1}$,
$\sin \theta = \frac{d}{-1}, \supset$, shown as.

$\sin x$ generates a circle of radius '1'.

### (b) Cosine function

Here, $f(x) = \cos x$

The domain of cosine function is $\mathbb{R}$ and the range is $[-1, 1]$.

#### Graph diagram (on $x$-axis and $y$-axis)
[ A graph showing a cosine wave with amplitude 1, period $2\pi$, and phase shift 0. The graph passes through the points $(-\pi, -1)$, $(0, 1)$, $(\pi, -1)$, and $(2\pi, 1)$. ]

As discussed, $\cos x$ decreases strictly from 1 to -1 as $x$ increases from 0 to $\pi$, increases strictly from -1 to 1 as $x$ increases from $\pi$ to $2\pi$ and so on. Also, $\cos x$ is periodic with period $2\pi$.

## Circle Diagram
Let a circle of radius '1', i.e., a unit circle.

Then, 
$\cos = \frac{a}{1}$, $\cos = \frac{b}{1}$,
$\cos = \frac{c}{1}$, $\cos = \frac{d}{1}$

$\cos x$ generates a circle of **radius '1'**.

[ A diagram showing a unit circle with points a, b, c, and d marked on it. The circle has a radius of 1 and is centered at the origin. The x and y axes are labeled. ]

### (c) Tangent Function

$f(x) = \tan x$

The domain of the function $y = \tan x$ is;

$R \quad \pm \frac{\pi}{2}, \pm \frac{3\pi}{2}, \pm \frac{5\pi}{2}, \ldots$

i.e., $R \quad (2n + 1) \frac{\pi}{2}$

and Range $R$ or $( -\infty, \infty )$.

The function $y = \tan x$ increases strictly from $-\infty$ to $+\infty$ as $x$ increases from 
$\frac{-\pi}{2} \neq \frac{\pi}{2}, \frac{3\pi}{2} \neq \frac{5\pi}{2}$, and so on.

[ A graph showing the tangent function with vertical asymptotes at $x = -\frac{\pi}{2}, \frac{\pi}{2}, \frac{3\pi}{2}$. The graph has a period of $\pi$ and increases from $-\infty$ to $+\infty$ in each period. ]

Note: Here, the curve tends to meet at $x=\pm \frac{\pi}{2}, \pm \frac{3\pi}{2}, \pm \frac{5\pi}{2} \ldots$ but never meets or tends to infinity.

$x = \pm \frac{\pi}{2}, \pm \frac{3\pi}{2}, \pm \frac{5\pi}{2} \ldots$ are asymptotes to $y = \tan x$.

### (d) Cosecant Function

$f(x) = \cosec x$

[ A graph showing the cosecant function with vertical asymptotes at $x = 0, \pm \pi, \pm 2\pi$. The graph has a period of $2\pi$ and has a minimum value of 1 and a maximum value of -1. ]

The graph of $y = \cosec x$ is shown above.

--- Page 17 ---

## Domain and Range of Trigonometric Functions

### (e) Secant Function

The function $y = \sec x$ is defined as:

$$
f(x) = \sec x
$$

The domain of $y = \sec x$ is $\mathbb{R} \setminus \{(2n+1)\frac{\pi}{2} | n \in \mathbb{Z}\}$ and the range is $\mathbb{R} \setminus (-1, 1)$.

[Graph of $y = \sec x$, showing a periodic curve with vertical asymptotes at $x = \pm \frac{\pi}{2}, \pm \frac{3\pi}{2}, ...$ and a minimum value of 1 and a maximum value of -1]

The function $y = \sec x$ is periodic with period $2\pi$.

### Notes

* The curve $y = \cosec x$ tends to meet at $x = 0, \pm \pi, \pm 2\pi, ...$ at infinity, or $x = n\pi, n \in \mathbb{Z}$ are asymptote to $y = \cosec x$.
* The curve $y = \sec x$ tends to meet at $x = \pm \frac{\pi}{2}, \pm \frac{3\pi}{2}, ...$ at infinity. $x = \pm \frac{\pi}{2}, \pm \frac{3\pi}{2}, ...$ or $x = (2n + 1) \frac{\pi}{2}, n \in \mathbb{Z}$ are asymptote to $y = \sec x$.

### (f) Cotangent Function

The function $y = \cot x$ is defined as:

$$
f(x) = \cot x
$$

The domain of $y = \cot x$ is $\mathbb{R} \setminus \{n\pi | n \in \mathbb{Z}\}$ and the range is $\mathbb{R}$.

[Graph of $y = \cot x$, showing a periodic curve with vertical asymptotes at $x = 0, \pm \pi, ...$]

The function $y = \cot x$ is periodic with period $\pi$, and has $x = n\pi, n \in \mathbb{Z}$ as asymptotes.

--- Page 18 ---

## 2. Exponential Function

The exponential function is defined as $f(x) = a^x$, where $a > 0$, $a \neq 1$, and $x \in R$. The domain of the exponential function is $R$, and the range is $(0, \infty)$.

### Case I. $a > 1$

For $a > 1$, the function $f(x) = a^x$ is increasing, i.e., $f(x)$ is an increasing function on $R$. [Figure of $y = a^x$ with $a > 1$, passing through $(0,1)$ and increasing]

For example, $y = 2^x$, $y = 3^x$, and $y = 4^x$ have:
- $2^x < 3^x < 4^x$ for $x > 1$
- $2^x > 3^x > 4^x$ for $0 < x < 1$

[Figure of $y = 2^x$, $y = 3^x$, and $y = 4^x$, with $y = 2^x$ having the lowest curve and $y = 4^x$ having the highest curve, all passing through $(0,1)$]

### Case II. $0 < a < 1$

For $0 < a < 1$, the function $f(x) = a^x$ is decreasing, i.e., $f(x)$ is a decreasing function on $R$. [Figure of $y = a^x$ with $0 < a < 1$, passing through $(0,1)$ and decreasing]

In general, the exponential function increases or decreases as ($a > 1$) or ($0 < a < 1$) respectively.

## 3. Logarithmic Function (Inverse of Exponential)

The function $f(x) = \log_a x$, where $x > 0$ and $a > 0$, $a \neq 1$ is a logarithmic function. Thus, the domain of the logarithmic function is all real positive numbers, and their range is the set $R$ of all real numbers.

We have seen that $y = a^x$ is strictly increasing **when** $a > 1$ and strictly decreasing **when** $0 < a < 1$.

--- Page 19 ---

## Properties of Logarithmic Functions

The function $y = a^x$ is invertible. The inverse of this function is denoted by $\log_a x$, where $x \in \mathbb{R}$ and $y \in (0, \infty)$. Writing $y = \log_a x$ in place of $x = \log_a y$, we have $y = \log_a x$.

Thus, the logarithmic function is also known as the inverse of the exponential function.

### Properties of Logarithmic Functions

1. $\log_e (ab) = \log_e a + \log_e b \quad \{a, b > 0\}$
2. $\log_e \frac{a}{b} = \log_e a - \log_e b \quad \{a, b > 0\}$
3. $\log_e a^m = m \log_e a \quad \{a > 0 \text{ and } m \in \mathbb{R}\}$
4. $\log_a a = 1 \quad \{a > 0 \text{ and } a \neq 1\}$
5. $\log_b a = \frac{1}{m} \log_b a^m \quad \{a, b > 0 \text{ and } m \in \mathbb{R}\}$
6. $\log_b a = \frac{1}{\log_a b} \quad \{a, b > 0 \text{ and } a, b \neq 1\}$
7. $\log_b a = \frac{\log_m a}{\log_m b} \quad \{a, b > 0 \neq 1 \text{ and } m > 0\}$
8. $a^{\log_a m} = m \quad \{a, m > 0 \text{ and } a \neq 1\}$
9. $a^{\log_c b} = b^{\log_c a} \quad \{a, b, c > 0 \text{ and } c \neq 1\}$

### Inequalities

10. If $\log_m x > \log_m y$, then $x > y$ if $m > 1$ and $x < y$ if $0 < m < 1$.

### Graphical Representations

[The graph of $y = \log_a x$ for $0 < a < 1$ and $a > 1$ is a curve that approaches the $y$-axis as $x$ approaches $0$ from the right, and approaches the $x$-axis as $x$ approaches $\infty$.]

[The graph of $\log_m x$ for $m > 1$ is a curve that increases from $-\infty$ to $\infty$ as $x$ increases from $0$ to $\infty$.]

[The graph of $\log_m x$ for $0 < m < 1$ is a curve that decreases from $\infty$ to $-\infty$ as $x$ increases from $0$ to $\infty$.]

### Additional Properties

11. $\log_m a = b \iff a = m^b \quad \{a, m > 0; m \neq 1; b \in \mathbb{R}\}$
12. $\log_m a > b \iff a > m^b; \text{ if } m > 1$ and $a < m^b; \text{ if } 0 < m < 1$
13. $\log_m a < b \iff a < m^b; \text{ if } m > 1$ and $a > m^b; \text{ if } 0 < m < 1$

## Geometrical Curves

### Straight Line
The general equation of a straight line is $ax + by + c = 0$. 
We know that 
- when $x = 0$, $y = -\frac{c}{b}$
- when $y = 0$, $x = -\frac{c}{a}$

Joining the above points, we get the required straight line. [A graph showing a straight line with x and y intercepts at $(0, -\frac{c}{b})$ and $(-\frac{c}{a}, 0)$ respectively]

### Circle

We know that:
- (i) $x^2 + y^2 = a^2$ is a circle with centre $(0, 0)$ and radius $r$. [A graph showing a circle centered at the origin with radius $r$]
- (ii) $(x - a)^2 + (y - b)^2 = r^2$ is a circle with centre $(a, b)$ and radius $r$. [A graph showing a circle centered at $(a, b)$ with radius $r$]
- (iii) $x^2 + y^2 + 2gx + 2fy + c = 0$; centre $(-g, -f)$; radius $\sqrt{g^2 + f^2 - c}$. [A graph showing a circle centered at $(-g, -f)$ with radius $\sqrt{g^2 + f^2 - c}$]
- (iv) $(x - x_1)(x - x_2) + (y - y_1)(y - y_2) = 0$; End points of diameter are $(x_1, y_1)$ and $(x_2, y_2)$. [A graph showing a circle with diameter endpoints $(x_1, y_1)$ and $(x_2, y_2)$]

### Parabola

- (i) $y^2 = 4ax$
  - Vertex: $(0, 0)$
  - Focus: $(a, 0)$
  - Axis: $x$-axis or $y = 0$
  - Directrix: $x = -a$
- (ii) $y^2 = -4ax$
  - Vertex: $(0, 0)$
  - Focus: $(-a, 0)$
  - Axis: $x$-axis or $y = 0$
  - Directrix: $x = a$

### Parabolas and Ellipses

## Parabolas

### $x^2 = 4ay$

* Vertex: $(0, 0)$
* Focus: $(0, a)$
* Axis: $x = 0$ or $y$-axis
* Directrix: $y = -a$ 
* [A pink graph showing a parabola opening upwards with vertex at (0,0), focus at (0,a), and directrix at $y=-a$]

The equation for this parabola is $x^2 = 4ay$.

### $y^2 = 4ax$

* Vertex: $(0, 0)$
* Focus: $(a, 0)$
* Axis: $y = 0$ or $x$-axis
* Directrix: $x = -a$
* [A pink graph showing a parabola opening to the right with vertex at (0,0), focus at (a,0), and directrix at $x=-a$]

The equation for this parabola is $y^2 = 4ax$.

### $y^2 = -4ax$

* Vertex: $(0, 0)$
* Focus: $(-a, 0)$
* Axis: $y = 0$ or $x$-axis
* Directrix: $x = a$
* [A pink graph showing a parabola opening to the left with vertex at (0,0), focus at (-a,0), and directrix at $x=a$]

The equation for this parabola is $y^2 = -4ax$.

### $x^2 = -4ay$

* Vertex: $(0, 0)$
* Focus: $(0, -a)$
* Axis: $x = 0$ or $y$-axis
* Directrix: $y = a$
* [A pink graph showing a parabola opening downwards with vertex at (0,0), focus at (0,-a), and directrix at $y=a$]

The equation for this parabola is $x^2 = -4ay$.

### $(y - k)^2 = 4a(x - h)$

* Vertex: $(h, k)$
* Focus: $(h + a, k)$
* Axis: $y = k$
* Directrix: $x = h - a$
* [A pink graph showing a parabola opening to the right with vertex at (h,k), focus at (h+a,k), and directrix at $x=h-a$]

The equation for this parabola is $(y - k)^2 = 4a(x - h)$.

## Ellipse

### $\frac{x^2}{a^2} + \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1$ ($a^2 > b^2$)

* Centre: $(0, 0)$
* Focus: $(\pm ae, 0)$
* Vertex: $(\pm a, 0)$
* Eccentricity: $e = \sqrt{1 - \frac{b^2}{a^2}}$
* Directrix: $x = \pm \frac{a}{e}$
* [A pink graph showing an ellipse centered at (0,0) with major axis $2a$ and minor axis $2b$]

The equation for this ellipse is $\frac{x^2}{a^2} + \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1$.

--- Page 22 ---

## Ellipse

### Equation and Properties

The equation of an ellipse can be represented as:

$\frac{x^2}{a^2} + \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1$ ($a^2 < b^2$)

*   Centre: $(0, 0)$
*   [Figure of ellipse with major axis along y-axis and minor axis along x-axis]

or

$\frac{(x - h)^2}{a^2} + \frac{(y - k)^2}{b^2} = 1$ ($a^2 > b^2$)

*   Centre: $(h, k)$
*   [Figure of ellipse with major axis along y-axis and minor axis along x-axis, centered at (h,k)]

## Hyperbola

### Equation and Properties

The equation of a hyperbola can be represented as:

$\frac{x^2}{a^2} - \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1$

*   Centre: $(0, 0)$
*   Focus: $(\pm ae, 0)$
*   Vertices: $(\pm a, 0)$
*   Eccentricity: $e = \sqrt{1 + \frac{b^2}{a^2}}$
*   Directrix: $x = \pm \frac{a}{e}$
*   Asymptotes: $y = \pm \frac{b}{a} x$
*   [Figure of hyperbola with transverse axis along x-axis]

or

$\frac{x^2}{a^2} + \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1$ 

No, this equation represents an ellipse.

$\frac{(x - h)^2}{a^2} - \frac{(y - k)^2}{b^2} = 1$

*   Centre: $(h, k)$
*   [Figure of hyperbola with transverse axis along x-axis, centered at (h,k)]

### Inverse Trigonometric Curves

As we know **trigonometric functions are many one** in their domain, hence, they are not **invertible**.

But their inverse can be obtained by restricting the domain so as to make invertible.

**Note**: Every inverse trigonometric is been converted to a function by shortening the domain.

### For Example:

Let $f(x) = \sin x$

We know, $\sin x$ is not invertible for $x \in R$. 

In order to get the inverse we have to define domain as:

If $f : \left[ -\frac{\pi}{2}, \frac{\pi}{2} \right] \to [-1, 1]$ defined by $f(x) = \sin x$ is invertible and inverse can be represented by:

$y = \sin^{-1} x \iff -\frac{\pi}{2} \le \sin^{-1} x \le \frac{\pi}{2}$

Similarly,

* $y = \cos x$ becomes invertible when $f : [0, \pi] \to [-1, 1]$
* $y = \tan x$ becomes invertible when $f : \left( -\frac{\pi}{2}, \frac{\pi}{2} \right) \to R$
* $y = \cot x$ becomes invertible when $f : (0, \pi) \to R$
* $y = \sec x$ becomes invertible when $f : [0, \pi] - \left\{ \frac{\pi}{2} \right\} \to (-\infty, -1] \cup [1, \infty)$
* $y = \cosec x$ becomes invertible when $f : \left[ -\frac{\pi}{2}, \frac{\pi}{2} \right] - \{0\} \to (-\infty, -1] \cup [1, \infty)$

--- Page 24 ---

### Inverse Trigonometric Functions and Their Graphs

#### (i) Graph of $y = \sin^{-1} x$

The domain of $y = \sin^{-1} x$ is $x \in [-1, 1]$ and the range is $y \in \left[-\frac{\pi}{2}, \frac{\pi}{2}\right]$. 
The graph of $y = \sin^{-1} x$ is the mirror image of $f(x) = \sin x$ about $y = x$. [A graph showing $y = \sin^{-1} x$, $y = x$, and $f(x) = \sin x$ with key points labeled: $(-1, -\frac{\pi}{2})$, $(0, 0)$, and $(1, \frac{\pi}{2})$].

#### (ii) Graph of $y = \cos^{-1} x$

The domain of $y = \cos^{-1} x$ is $x \in [-1, 1]$ and the range is $y \in [0, \pi]$. 
[A graph showing $y = \cos^{-1} x$, $y = x$, and $f(x) = \cos x$ with key points labeled].

#### (iii) Graph of $y = \tan^{-1} x$

The domain of $y = \tan^{-1} x$ is $x \in \mathbb{R}$ and the range is $y \in \left(-\frac{\pi}{2}, \frac{\pi}{2}\right)$. 
[A graph showing $y = \tan^{-1} x$ and $y = \tan x$ with vertical asymptotes at $x = -\frac{\pi}{2}$ and $x = \frac{\pi}{2}$].

#### (iv) Graph of $y = \cot^{-1} x$

The domain of $y = \cot^{-1} x$ is $x \in \mathbb{R}$ and the range is $y \in (0, \pi)$. 
The function $f : (0, \pi) \to \mathbb{R}$ given by $f(x) = \cot x$ is invertible. 
[A graph showing $y = \cot^{-1} x$ and $y = \cot x$ with a vertical asymptote at $x = 0$].

As discussed earlier, the graph of an inverse function is the mirror image of $f(x)$ about $y = x$ or obtained by interchanging the coordinate axes.

--- Page 25 ---

### Inverse Trigonometric Functions

#### Graph for $y = \sec^{-1} x$

The function $f : [0, \frac{\pi}{2}) \cup (\frac{\pi}{2}, \pi] \to [1, \infty)$ given by $f(x) = \sec x$ is invertible.

$y = \sec^{-1} x$ has domain $\mathbb{R} \setminus (-1, 1)$ and range $[0, \pi] \setminus \{\frac{\pi}{2}\}$.

[The graph of $y = \sec x$ is a curve that approaches the $y$-axis asymptotically, with a minimum value of $1$ at $x=0$. The graph of $y = \sec^{-1} x$ is a curve that starts at $(1, 0)$ and increases to $\infty$ as $x$ approaches $\infty$.]

#### Graph for $y = \cosec^{-1} x$

As we know, $f : [-\frac{\pi}{2}, 0) \cup (0, \frac{\pi}{2}] \to \mathbb{R} \setminus (-1, 1)$ is invertible given by $f(x) = \cosec x$.

$y = \cosec^{-1} x$; domain $\mathbb{R} \setminus (-1, 1)$

Range $[-\frac{\pi}{2}, \frac{\pi}{2}] \setminus \{0\}$.

[The graph of $y = \cosec x$ is a curve that approaches the $x$-axis asymptotically, with a maximum value of $1$ at $x=\frac{\pi}{2}$ and a minimum value of $-1$ at $x=-\frac{\pi}{2}$. The graph of $y = \cosec^{-1} x$ is a curve that starts at $(-1, -\frac{\pi}{2})$ and decreases to $-\infty$ as $x$ approaches $0$ from the left, and starts at $(1, \frac{\pi}{2})$ and decreases to $\infty$ as $x$ approaches $0$ from the right.]

**Note**: If no branch of an inverse trigonometric function is mentioned, then it means the principal value branch of that function. In case no branch of an inverse trigonometric function is mentioned, it will mean the principal value branch of that function. (i.e.,)

## Inverse Trigonometric Functions

### Table of Inverse Trigonometric Functions

| Function | Domain | Range | Principal Value Branch |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| $\sin^{-1} x$ | $[-1, 1]$ | $\left[ -\frac{\pi}{2}, \frac{\pi}{2} \right]$ | $-\frac{\pi}{2} \leq y \leq \frac{\pi}{2}$, where $y = \sin^{-1} x$ |
| $\cos^{-1} x$ | $[-1, 1]$ | $[0, \pi]$ | $0 \leq y \leq \pi$, where $y = \cos^{-1} x$ |
| $\tan^{-1} x$ | $\mathbb{R}$ | $\left( -\frac{\pi}{2}, \frac{\pi}{2} \right)$ | $-\frac{\pi}{2} < y < \frac{\pi}{2}$, where $y = \tan^{-1} x$ |
| $\cosec^{-1} x$ | $(- \infty, -1] \cup [1, \infty)$ | $\left[ -\frac{\pi}{2}, \frac{\pi}{2} \right] - \{0\}$ | $-\frac{\pi}{2} \leq y \leq \frac{\pi}{2}; y \neq 0$, where $y = \cosec^{-1} x$ |
| $\sec^{-1} x$ | $(- \infty, -1] \cup [1, \infty)$ | $[0, \pi] - \left\{ \frac{\pi}{2} \right\}$ | $0 \leq y \leq \pi; y \neq \frac{\pi}{2}$, where $y = \sec^{-1} x$ |
| $\cot^{-1} x$ | $\mathbb{R}$ | $(0, \pi)$ | $0 < y < \pi$, where $y = \cot^{-1} x$ |

## 1.3 Trigonometric Inequalities

To solve trigonometric inequalities including trigonometric functions, it is good to practice periodicity and monotonicity of functions.

Thus, first solve the inequality for the periodicity and then get the set of all solutions by adding numbers of the form $2n\pi; n \in \mathbb{Z}$, to each of the solutions obtained on that interval.

### Example 1: Solve the inequality $\sin x > -\frac{1}{2}$.

#### Solution
As the function $\sin x$ has least positive period $2\pi$. {That is why it is sufficient to solve inequality of the form $\sin x > a, \sin x \geq a, \sin x < a, \sin x \leq a$ first on the interval of length $2\pi$, and then get the solution set by adding numbers of the form $2n\pi, n \in \mathbb{Z}$, to each of the solutions obtained on that interval}. Thus, let us solve this inequality on the interval $\left[ -\frac{\pi}{2}, \frac{3\pi}{2} \right]$, where graph of $y = \sin x$ and $y = -\frac{1}{2}$ are taken two curves on $x-y$ plane.

[The graph shows two curves, $y = \sin x$ and $y = -\frac{1}{2}$, on the $x-y$ plane, intersecting at $x = -\frac{\pi}{6}$ and $x = \frac{7\pi}{6}$. The curve $y = \sin x$ is above the line $y = -\frac{1}{2}$ for $x \in \left( -\frac{\pi}{6}, \frac{7\pi}{6} \right)$.]

$y = \sin x$ and $y = -\frac{1}{2}$

#### Example 5: Solve the Inequality $\cos 2x \sin 2x \geq 0$

The expression $\cos 2x \sin 2x$ can be reduced to,
$$
\begin{aligned}
\sqrt{2} \cdot \frac{1}{\sqrt{2}} \sin 2x \cos 2x &= \sqrt{2} \cos \frac{\pi}{4} \cos 2x + \sin \frac{\pi}{4} \sin 2x \\
&= \sqrt{2} \cos \left(\frac{\pi}{4} - 2x\right).
\end{aligned}
$$

$\cos 2x \sin 2x \geq 0$ or $\cos \left(\frac{\pi}{4} - 2x\right) \geq 0$; put $2x + \frac{\pi}{4} = t$

$\cos(t) \geq 0$, solving graphically,

\end{aligned}
$$

$$

t = 2n + \frac{\pi}{4} 
\end{aligned}
$$

$$
\begin{aligned}
2n\pi - \frac{\pi}{2} < 2n + \frac{\pi}{4} < 2n\pi + \frac{\pi}{2}

$\cos A + \cos B + \cos C < 3 \cos \frac{\pi}{3}$

$\cos A + \cos B + \cos C < \frac{3}{2}$

### Note
Here, a particular case arises when $A = B = C$ (i.e., when $A, B, C$ are non-distinct)

$\cos A = \cos B = \cos C$ and $A + B + C = \pi$

$A + A + A = \pi$ or $A = \pi/3$.

$\cos A = \cos B = \cos C = \frac{1}{2}$

$\cos A + \cos B + \cos C = \frac{3}{2}$ (only when $A = B = C$)

### Example 7
Solve the inequality: $\sin x \cos x + \frac{1}{2} \tan x \geq 1$.

## Solution
Here; left-hand side is defined for all $x$, except $x = n\pi + \frac{\pi}{2}$, where $n \in z$.

$2 \sin x \cos x + \tan x \geq 2$

$\frac{2 \tan x}{1 + \tan^2 x} + \tan x \geq 2$

$\frac{2y}{1+y^2} + y \geq 2$ [Let, $\tan x = y$]

--- Page 30 ---

## Step 1: Convert the inequality to LaTeX
The given inequality is $\frac{2y + y(1+y^2)}{2(1+y^2)} \geq 0$. This can be written in LaTeX as $\frac{2y + y(1+y^2)}{2(1+y^2)} \geq 0$ or $\frac{y(3+y^2)}{2(1+y^2)} \geq 0$. Since $1+y^2 \geq 0$, we have $y(3+y^2) \geq 0$. 

## 2: Factor the inequality
The inequality $y(3+y^2) \geq 0$ can be analyzed by finding its roots. $y^3 + 3y \geq 0$ or $y(y^2 + 3) \geq 0$. Since $y^2 + 3 > 0$ for all $y$, we require $y \geq 0$.

## 3: Describe the graph/figure in brackets
The graph of $\tan x \geq 1$ is shown [a graph of the tangent function with the region above the line $y=1$ shaded, with a vertical asymptote at $x=-\pi/2$ and the curve intersecting the line $y=1$ at $x=\pi/4$].

## 4: Write the conditions for $x$ and $n$
The conditions are $x < \frac{\pi}{2}$ and $n \neq \frac{\pi}{4}$ or $x < n < \frac{\pi}{2}$; $n \in \mathbb{Z}$.

## 5: Transcribe example 8 into clean Markdown
### Example 8
If $A + B + C = \pi$, then prove that $\tan^2 \frac{A}{2} + \tan^2 \frac{B}{2} + \tan^2 \frac{C}{2} > 1$.

## 6: Provide the solution in Markdown
### Solution
Here, $\tan^2 \frac{A}{2}$, $\tan^2 \frac{B}{2}$, and $\tan^2 \frac{C}{2}$ are three same function. So consider $f(x) = \tan^2 \frac{x}{2}$, whose period is $2\pi$. 

Plotting $\tan^2 \frac{x}{2}$ for $x \in (-\pi, \pi)$.

In given curve let us consider any three points $A$, $B$, $C$ such that $A + B + C = \pi$.

Now, centroid of $\triangle RST$; $G(\frac{A+B+C}{3}, \frac{\tan^2 \frac{A}{2} + \tan^2 \frac{B}{2} + \tan^2 \frac{C}{2}}{3})$.

Also, $M(\frac{A+B+C}{3}, \tan^2 \frac{A+B+C}{2(3)})$.

$\frac{\tan^2 \frac{A}{2} + \tan^2 \frac{B}{2} + \tan^2 \frac{C}{2}}{3} > \tan^2 \frac{A+B+C}{6}$.

$\tan^2 \frac{A}{2} + \tan^2 \frac{B}{2} + \tan^2 \frac{C}{2} > 1$.

The graph of the problem is [a graph with three curves and a triangle formed by connecting points on the curves, with a line through the centroid and the point $M$].

## Some More Solved Examples

### Example 1: Sketch the graph for $y = \sin^{-1} (\sin x)$

#### Solution
As, $y = \sin^{-1} (\sin x)$ is periodic with period $2\pi$.

To draw this graph we should draw the graph for one interval of length $2\pi$ and repeat for entire values of $x$.

As we know;

$$
\sin^{-1} (\sin x) =
\begin{cases}
x; & -\frac{\pi}{2} \leq x \leq \frac{\pi}{2} \\
(\pi - x); & \frac{\pi}{2} \leq x < \frac{3\pi}{2}
\end{cases}
$$

or

$$
\sin^{-1} (\sin x) =
\begin{cases}
x; & -\frac{\pi}{2} \leq x \leq \frac{\pi}{2} \\
\pi - x; & \frac{\pi}{2} \leq x \leq \frac{3\pi}{2},
\end{cases}
$$

which is defined for the interval of length $2\pi$, plotted as; 

[The graph consists of a series of connected line segments with a period of $2\pi$. The main curve is a straight line from $(-\frac{\pi}{2}, -\frac{\pi}{2})$ to $(\frac{\pi}{2}, \frac{\pi}{2})$. This pattern repeats, creating a sawtooth-like graph.]

Thus, the graph for $y = \sin^{-1} (\sin x)$, is a straight line up and a straight line down with slopes 1 and -1 respectively lying between $-\frac{\pi}{2}$ and $\frac{\pi}{2}$.

**Note**: Students are advised to learn the definition of $\sin^{-1} (\sin x)$ as,

$$
y = \sin^{-1} (\sin x) =
\begin{cases}
x + 2\pi; & -\frac{5\pi}{2} \leq x \leq -\frac{3\pi}{2} \\
-x; & -\frac{3\pi}{2} \leq x \leq -\frac{\pi}{2} \\
x; & -\frac{\pi}{2} \leq x \leq \frac{\pi}{2} \\
\pi - x; & \frac{\pi}{2} \leq x \leq \frac{3\pi}{2} \\
x - 2\pi; & \frac{3\pi}{2} \leq x \leq \frac{5\pi}{2} 
\end{cases}
$$ 
... and so on.

--- Page 34 ---

### EXAMPLE 2
Sketch the graph for $y = \cos^{-1} (\cos x)$.

### SOLUTION
As, $y = \cos^{-1} (\cos x)$ is periodic with period $2\pi$.

As we know;
$$
\cos^{-1} (\cos x) =
\begin{cases}
x; & 0 \le x \le \pi \\
2\pi - x; & 0 \le 2\pi - x \le \pi,
\end{cases}
$$
or 
$$
\cos^{-1} (\cos x) =
\begin{cases}
x; & 0 \le x \le \pi \\
2\pi - x; & \pi \le x \le 2\pi.
\end{cases}
$$

Thus, it has been defined for $0 < x < 2\pi$ that has length $2\pi$. So, its graph could be plotted as; 

[ A graph of a periodic function with a period of $2 \pi$. The graph is a straight line with a positive slope from $(0,0)$ to $(\pi, \pi)$, then a straight line with a negative slope from $(\pi, \pi)$ to $(2 \pi, 0)$. The graph repeats every $2 \pi$ units. ]

Thus, the curve $y = \cos^{-1} (\cos x)$.

### EXAMPLE 3
Sketch the graph for $y = \tan^{-1} (\tan x)$.

### SOLUTION
As $y = \tan^{-1} (\tan x)$ is periodic with period $\pi$.

to draw this graph we should draw the graph for one interval of length $\pi$ and repeat for entire values of $x$.

As we know;
$$
\tan^{-1} (\tan x) = x; -\frac{\pi}{2} < x < \frac{\pi}{2}
$$

Thus, it has been defined for 
$-\frac{\pi}{2} < x < \frac{\pi}{2}$ that has length $\pi$. So, its graph could be plotted as;

[ A graph of a periodic function with a period of $\pi$. The graph is a straight line with a positive slope from $(-\frac{\pi}{2}, -\frac{\pi}{2})$ to $(\frac{\pi}{2}, \frac{\pi}{2})$. The graph repeats every $\pi$ units, with vertical asymptotes at $x = \frac{\pi}{2} + k \pi$, where $k$ is an integer. ]

Thus, the curve for $y = \tan^{-1} (\tan x)$, where $y$ is not defined for $x = (2n + 1) \frac{\pi}{2}$.

--- Page 35 ---

### EXAMPLE 4: Sketch the graph for $y = \cosec^{-1}(\cosec x)$.

#### SOLUTION
As $y = \cosec^{-1}(\cosec x)$ is periodic with period $2\pi$. To draw this graph, we should draw the graph for one interval of length $2\pi$ and repeat for entire values of $x$.

As we know,
$$
\begin{aligned}
\cosec^{-1}(\cosec x) &= 
\begin{cases}
x; & -\frac{\pi}{2} < x < 0 \quad \text{or} \quad 0 < x < \frac{\pi}{2} \\
-x; & \frac{\pi}{2} < x < 0 \quad \text{or} \quad 0 < x < \frac{3\pi}{2}
\end{cases}
\\
\text{or} \\
\cosec^{-1}(\cosec x) &= 
\begin{cases}
x; & x \in \left[ -\frac{\pi}{2}, \frac{\pi}{2} \right] \\
-x; & x \in \left[ \frac{\pi}{2}, \frac{3\pi}{2} \right]
\end{cases}
\end{aligned}
$$

Thus, it has been defined for $\left[ -\frac{\pi}{2}, \frac{3\pi}{2} \right] \backslash \{0, \pi\}$ that has length $2\pi$. So, its graph could be plotted as 
[ A graph of a periodic function with period $2\pi$, with a V-shaped graph that has its minimum at $(\frac{\pi}{2}, -\frac{\pi}{2})$ and $(\frac{3\pi}{2}, -\frac{\pi}{2})$, and maximum at $(-\frac{\pi}{2}, \frac{\pi}{2})$ and $(\frac{\pi}{2}, \frac{\pi}{2})$ and similar shape repeating. The graph is symmetric about the origin. ].

### EXAMPLE 5: Sketch the graph for $y = \sec^{-1}(\sec x)$.

#### SOLUTION
As $y = \sec^{-1}(\sec x)$ is periodic with period $2\pi$. To draw this graph, we should draw the graph for one interval of length $2\pi$ and repeat for entire values of $x$.

As we know,
$$
\begin{aligned}
\sec^{-1}(\sec x) &= 
\begin{cases}
x; & x \in \left[ 0, \frac{\pi}{2} \right) \quad \text{or} \quad \left( \frac{\pi}{2}, \pi \right] \\
2\pi - x; & x \in \left( \pi, \frac{3\pi}{2} \right) \quad \text{or} \quad \left( \frac{3\pi}{2}, 2\pi \right]
\end{cases}
\\
\text{or} \\
\sec^{-1}(\sec x) &= 
\begin{cases}
x; & 0 < x < \frac{\pi}{2} \quad \text{or} \quad \frac{3\pi}{2} < x < 2\pi \\
2\pi - x; & \pi < x < \frac{3\pi}{2}
\end{cases}
\end{aligned}
$$

Thus, it has been defined for $[0, 2\pi] \backslash \{\frac{\pi}{2}, \frac{3\pi}{2}\}$ that has length $2\pi$. So, its graph could be plotted as 
[ A graph of a periodic function with period $2\pi$, with a V-shaped graph that has its minimum at $(\pi, \pi)$ and maximum at $(0, 0)$ and similar shape repeating. The graph is symmetric about the line $x = \pi$. ].

## EXAMPLE 8: Sketch the graph for:

### (i) $\sin^{-1} \frac{2x}{1+x^2}$

### (ii) $\cos^{-1} \frac{1-x^2}{1+x^2}$

### (iii) $\tan^{-1} \frac{2x}{1-x^2}$

### (iv) $\tan^{-1} \frac{3x-x^3}{1-3x^2}$

### (v) $\sin^{-1} (3x-4x^3)$

### (vi) $\cos^{-1} (4x^3-3x)$.

## SOLUTION

As we know, all the above-mentioned six curves are non-periodic, but have **restricted domain and range**. So, we shall first define each curve for its domain and range and then sketch these curves.

### (i) Sketch for $y = \sin^{-1} \frac{2x}{1+x^2}$

Here, for domain

$$
\left|\frac{2x}{1+x^2}\right| \leq 1
$$

$$
2|x| \leq 1+x^2 \quad \{\because 1+x^2 > 0 \text{ for all } x\}
$$

$$
|x|^2 - 2|x| + 1 \geq 0
$$

$$
(|x|-1)^2 \geq 0
$$

$$
x \in R.
$$

For range:

$$
y = \sin^{-1} \frac{2x}{1+x^2}
$$

$$
y \in \left[-\frac{\pi}{2}, \frac{\pi}{2}\right] \text{ as } y = \sin^{-1} y \in \left[-\frac{\pi}{2}, \frac{\pi}{2}\right]
$$

Defining the curve: Let, $x = \tan \theta$

$$
\begin{aligned}
&\neq \frac{\pi}{2} ; \frac{\pi}{2} > \frac{\pi}{4} \\
&y = \sin^{-1} (\sin 2\theta) = 2\theta ; -\frac{\pi}{2} \leq 2\theta \leq \frac{\pi}{2} \\
&\neq \frac{\pi}{2} ; 2\theta < \frac{\pi}{2} \\
&\neq 2\tan^{-1} x ; \tan^{-1} x > \frac{\pi}{4} \\
&y = 2\tan^{-1} x ; -\frac{\pi}{4} \leq \tan^{-1} x \leq \frac{\pi}{4} \{\because \tan \theta = x \Rightarrow \theta = \tan^{-1} x\} \\
&\neq 2\tan^{-1} x ; \tan^{-1} x < -\frac{\pi}{4} \\
&\neq 2\tan^{-1} x ; x > 1 \\
&y = 2\tan^{-1} x ; -1 < x < 1 \\
&\neq 2\tan^{-1} x ; x < -1 \\
\end{aligned}
$$

Thus, $y = \sin^{-1} \frac{2x}{1+x^2}$ is defined for $x \in R$, where $y \in \left[-\frac{\pi}{2}, \frac{\pi}{2}\right]$, so the graph for Eq. (i) could be shown as [a graph with a specific shape].

[Graph of $y = \sin^{-1} \frac{2x}{1+x^2}$]

## Introduction of Graphs

### Graphs and Equations

The graph for $y = \sin^{-1} \frac{2x}{1+x^2}$ is shown below [A graph showing a curve with sharp edges at $x = -1$ and $x = 1$, and horizontal asymptotes at $y = \frac{\pi}{2}$ and $y = -\frac{\pi}{2}$].

Thus, the graph for $y = \sin^{-1} \frac{2x}{1+x^2}$.

### Note

As in later sections (i.e., chapter 2) we shall discuss that functions having sharp edges and gaps are not differentiable at that point.

So, in the previous curve $y = \sin^{-1} \frac{2x}{1+x^2}$, we know it has a sharp edge at $x = 1$ and $x = -1$. So, not differentiable.

### Sketch for $y = \cos^{-1} \frac{1-x^2}{1+x^2}$

#### Domain

$\left| \frac{1-x^2}{1+x^2} \right| \leq 1$

$\left| 1 - x^2 \right| \leq 1+x^2$

which is true for all $x$; as $1 + x^2 > 1 - x^2$

$x \in R$

#### Range

$y = \cos^{-1} \frac{1-x^2}{1+x^2}$; $y \in (0, \pi)$

#### Define the Curve

Let $x = \tan \theta$

$y = \cos^{-1} \frac{1-\tan^2 \theta}{1+\tan^2 \theta} = \cos^{-1} (\cos 2\theta)$

$= \begin{cases} 2\theta; & 2\theta \geq 0 \\ -2\theta; & 2\theta < 0 \end{cases}$

$y = \begin{cases} 2\tan^{-1} x; & \tan^{-1} x \geq 0 \\ -2\tan^{-1} x; & \tan^{-1} x < 0 \end{cases}$ $\{\because \tan \theta = x \Rightarrow \theta = \tan^{-1} x\}$

#### Trigonometric Identities

The following trigonometric identities hold:

* $y = \cos x \sec x = 1$; $x \in R - (2n + 1) \frac{\pi}{2}$; $n \in Z$
* $y = \tan x \cot x = 1$; $x \in R - n\pi, n \neq \frac{\pi}{2}$; $n \in Z$

**Note**: Although $y = \sin x \csc x = 1$, $y = \cos x \sec x = 1$, and $y = \tan x \cot x = 1$, they are not equal due to different domains.

**Equal functions**: Functions with the same domain and range are considered equal.

### Equation 1
The function $y = \frac{|\sin x|}{\sin x}$ can be defined as:
$$
y =
\begin{cases}

-1; & (2n + 1)\pi < x < (2n + 2)\pi; \; n \; \in \; \mathbb{Z}
\end{cases}
$$

### Domain and Range
- **Domain:** $\mathbb{R} - \{n\pi; \; n \; \in \; \mathbb{Z}\}$
- **Range:**
  - $1; \; 2n\pi < x < (2n + 1)\pi$
  - $-1; \; (2n + 1)\pi < x < (2n + 2)\pi$

[Figure 1.92: A graph showing a step function with $y = 1$ for $2n\pi < x < (2n + 1)\pi$ and $y = -1$ for $(2n + 1)\pi < x < (2n + 2)\pi$]

### Equation 2
The function $y = \frac{|\cos x|}{\cos x}$ can be defined as:
$$
y =
\begin{cases}
1; & \cos x > 0 \\
-1; & \cos x < 0
\end{cases}
$$
More specifically:
$$
y =
\begin{cases}
1; & 2n\pi - \frac{\pi}{2} < x < 2n\pi + \frac{\pi}{2} \\
-1; & 2n\pi + \frac{\pi}{2} < x < 2n\pi + \frac{3\pi}{2}
\end{cases}
$$

[Figure 1.93: A graph showing a step function with $y = 1$ for $2n\pi - \frac{\pi}{2} < x < 2n\pi + \frac{\pi}{2}$ and $y = -1$ for $2n\pi + \frac{\pi}{2} < x < 2n\pi + \frac{3\pi}{2}$]

### Equation 3
The function $y = \sin^{-1} \frac{1 + x^2}{2x}$ is defined when:
$$
\left|\frac{1 + x^2}{2x}\right| \leq 1
$$
This implies:
$$
1 + x^2 \leq 2|x|
$$
$$
x^2 - 2|x| + 1 \leq 0
$$
$$
(|x| - 1)^2 \leq 0
$$
Since $(|x| - 1)^2 < 0$ is not possible, we have:
$$
(|x| - 1)^2 = 0
$$

### EXAMPLE 16

Find the number of solutions of 
$$ 
4\{x\} = x + [x]
$$
where $\{ \}$, $[\ ]$ represents fractional part and greatest integer function.

### SOLUTION

As we know, to find number of solutions of two curves we should find the point of intersection of two curves.

$$
\begin{aligned}
4\{x\} &= x + [x] \\
4(x - [x]) &= x + [x] \\
\{ \because x = [x] + \{x\} \} \\
4x - x &= 4[x] + [x] \\
3x &= 5 [x] \\
[x] &= \frac{3}{5} x 
\end{aligned}
$$
... (i)

To plot the graph of both 
$$
y = [x] 
$$
and 
$$
y = \frac{3}{5} x
$$
[Two graphs, one a step function with steps at integer values of x and the other a straight line with slope 3/5, intersecting at two points]

Clearly, the two graphs intersect when 
$$
[x] = 0 
$$
and 
$$
[x] = 1
$$
$$
x = \frac{5}{3} [x] 
$$
[from Eqs. (i) and (ii)]

$$
x = \frac{5}{3} \cdot 0 
$$
and 
$$
x = \frac{5}{3} \cdot 1
$$

$$
x = 0 
$$
and 
$$
x = \frac{5}{3}
$$

are the only two solutions.

### EXAMPLE 17

Find the value of $x$ graphically satisfying; $[x] - 1 + x^2 \geq 0$; where $[ \ ]$ denotes the greatest integer function.

### SOLUTION

As,
$$
[x] - 1 + x^2 \geq 0 
$$
and 
$$
x^2 - 1 \geq -[x]
$$

Thus, to find the points for which $f(x) = x^2 - 1$ is greater than or equal to 
$$
g(x) = -[x]
$$
where two functions $f(x)$ and $g(x)$ could be plotted as shown in [a graph with a parabola and a step function, with shaded regions indicating where the parabola is greater than or equal to the step function].

From the adjoining figure; the solution set lies when 
$$
x \in A 
$$
or 
$$
x \geq B
$$

Thus, to find $A$ and $B$.

It is clear that $f(x)$ and $g(x)$ intersects when; 
$$
-[x] = 2
$$

$$
\begin{aligned}
x^2 - 1 &= 2 \\
x^2 &= 3 \\
x &= \pm \sqrt{3} 
\end{aligned}
$$
$$
x = -\sqrt{3}
$$
(neglecting $x = +\sqrt{3}$ as $A$ lies for $x < 0$)

## EXAMPLE 20
If $A + B + C = \pi$ and $A, B, C$ are angles of $\triangle$, then show 
$$
\sin A + \sin B + \sin C < \frac{3\sqrt{3}}{2}.
$$

### SOLUTION
Here, we have three trigonometric ratios $\sin A, \sin B, \sin C$.

Let $y = \sin x$, on which there are three points $x = A, x = B$ and $x = C$ shown as:
As from the figure; In $\triangle PQR$, 
Centroid of $\triangle$ formed by $P(A, \sin A) Q(B, \sin B) R(C, \sin C)$ is;
$$
G = \frac{A + B + C}{3}, \frac{\sin A + \sin B + \sin C}{3}
$$
where; $G, H$ and $I$ are collinear 

$$
I \frac{A + B + C}{3} , 0 ; G \frac{A + B + C}{3} , \frac{\sin A + \sin B + \sin C}{3}
$$

and 
$$
H \frac{A + B + C}{3} , \sin \frac{A + B + C}{3}
$$

From figure; 
i.e., ordinate of $H>$ ordinate of $G$

$$
\sin \frac{A + B + C}{3} > \frac{\sin A + \sin B + \sin C}{3}
$$

$$
\frac{3\sqrt{3}}{2} > \sin A + \sin B + \sin C.
$$

### EXAMPLE 21

If $0 < A < \frac{\pi}{6}$, then show $A(\cosec A) < \frac{\pi}{3}$.

SOLUTION 
Here, graph for $y = \sin x$ is shown as; 
[The graph of $y = \sin x$ with points $P(A, \sin A)$ and $Q(\frac{\pi}{6}, \sin \frac{\pi}{6})$ marked. The line segments $OP$ and $OQ$ are drawn, where $O$ is the origin.]

From adjoining figure; 
slope of $OP >$ slope of $OQ$

$$
\frac{\sin A - 0}{A-0} > \frac{\sin \frac{\pi}{6} - 0}{\frac{\pi}{6} - 0}
$$

$$
\frac{\sin A}{A} > \frac{3}{\pi} 
$$

or 

$$
\frac{A}{\sin A} < \frac{\pi}{3}
$$

$$
A(\cosec A) < \frac{\pi}{3}.
$$

### EXAMPLE 22

If $0 < A, B, C < \frac{\pi}{2}$, then show that: 
$ A \cosec A + B \cosec B + C \cosec C < \frac{3\pi}{2}$.

SOLUTION 
Here, graph for $y = \sin x$ is shown as; 
[The graph of $y = \sin x$ with points $P(B, \sin B); Q(C, \sin C); R(A, \sin A); S(\frac{\pi}{2}, \sin \frac{\pi}{2})$ marked. The line segments $OP$, $OQ$, $OR$, and $OS$ are drawn, where $O$ is the origin.]

From figure; 
**slope of $OP >$ slope of $OS$**:

$$
\frac{\sin B - 0}{B - 0} > \frac{\sin \frac{\pi}{2} - 0}{\frac{\pi}{2} - 0}
$$

$$
\frac{\sin B}{B} > \frac{2}{\pi} 
$$

or 

$$
\frac{B}{\sin B} < \frac{\pi}{2}
$$

$$
B \cosec B < \frac{\pi}{2} 
$$
...(i)

Similarly, slope of $OQ <$ slope of $OS$ and slope of $OR <$ slope of $OS$.

$$
C \cosec C < \frac{\pi}{2} 
$$
...(ii)

$$
A \cosec A < \frac{\pi}{2} 
$$
...(iii)

Adding Eqs. (i), (ii) and (iii), we get

$$
A \cosec A + B \cosec B + C \cosec C < \frac{3\pi}{2}.
$$

**Note**: Students must practice the above method in different questions of trigonometric inequality as it saves time.

## Myths About Tangent Lines

### Remark 1
A line can meet a curve at a single point without being tangent to it. [A graph showing a line $L$ intersecting a curve $C$ at point $P$, but the line is not tangent to the curve.]

### Remark 2
A line can be tangent to a curve at a point but intersect the curve at multiple points. [A graph showing a line $L$ tangent to a curve $C$ at point $P$, but intersecting the curve at several other points.]

### Remark 3
A line can be tangent to a curve at a point and intersect the curve at that point, crossing from one side of the curve to the other. [A graph showing a line $L$ tangent to a curve $C$ at point $P$, crossing from one side of the curve to the other.]

## Curvature and Transformations

In this chapter we shall study:  
The bending of curves at different points.  
Transformations of curves.

### 2.1 Curvature

"The study of bending of curves at different points is known as curvature." or "Rate at which the curve curves".

Consider a curve and a point $P$ on it and let $Q$ be a point near $P$. Let $A$ be a point on the curve.

$\text{arc } AP = s, \quad \text{arc } AQ = s + \delta s$

$\text{arc } PQ = \delta s$

Let $\phi$ and $\phi + \delta \phi$ be angles which the tangents at $P$ and $Q$ makes with $x$-axis.

$\frac{\delta \phi}{\delta s}$ is called the **average curvature** of the arc $PQ$.

$\lim_{\delta s \to 0} \frac{\delta \phi}{\delta s} = \frac{d \phi}{ds}$ is called **curvature** of the curve at $P$.

[ A graph showing a curve with points $P$, $Q$, and $A$, and tangent lines at $P$ and $Q$, with the $x$-axis and $y$-axis labeled. ]

Note: To study curvature we shall define $\frac{d \phi}{ds}$ or $\frac{d^2y}{dx^2}$.

### 2.2 Concavity, Convexity and Points of Inflection

#### (a) Concave upwards

If in the neighbourhood of a point $P$ on a curve is above the tangent at $P$, it is said to be concave upwards.

Mathematically $\frac{dy}{dx}$ increases as $x$ increases.

$\frac{d^2y}{dx^2} > 0$

--- Page 59 ---

## Convex Upwards or Concave Downwards

If the curve is below the tangent at $P$, it is said to be convex upward or concave downward.

### Mathematically

$\frac{dy}{dx}$ decreases as $x$ decreases. $\frac{d^2y}{dx^2} < 0$

### Geometrically

[The curve is concave downward, with the tangent line above the curve at point $P$.]

## Point of Inflexion

If at a point $P$, a curve changes its concavity from upwards to downwards or vice versa. Then $P$ is called point of inflexion.

### Geometrically

[The curve changes from concave upward to concave downward at point $P$, or vice versa.]

### Mathematically

(i) $\frac{d^2y}{dx^2} = 0$ at the point.

(ii) $\frac{d^2y}{dx^2}$ changes its sign as $x$ increases through the value at which $\frac{d^2y}{dx^2} = 0$; i.e., $\frac{d^3y}{dx^3} \uparrow 0$.

--- Page 60 ---

## Plotting of Algebraic Curves Using Concavity

### Concavity Representation

In general, concavity can be represented as:

*   A curve is concave up if $\frac{d^2y}{dx^2} > 0$ [a graph showing a curve concave up with a tangent line, where the curve is below the tangent line].
*   A curve is concave down if $\frac{d^2y}{dx^2} < 0$ [a graph showing a curve concave down with a tangent line, where the curve is above the tangent line].

### Plotting Curves

To plot curves, we need to:

1.  Find the point of intersection on the $x$-axis (i.e., $y = 0$).
2.  Find the point of maximum and minimum value.
3.  Determine the interval for which the function increases or decreases.
4.  Find the point at which the curve is concave up, concave down, and the point of inflection.

### Example 1: Sketch $y = (x-1)(x-2)$

#### Solution

1.  Put $y = 0$: $x = 1, 2$ (point of intersection on $x$-axis).
2.  $y = x^2 - 3x + 2$.
    *   $\frac{dy}{dx} = 2x - 3$.
    *   $\frac{d^2y}{dx^2} = 2 > 0$, indicating a minimum at $x = \frac{3}{2}$.
3.  The function increases when $x > \frac{3}{2}$ and decreases when $x < \frac{3}{2}$ [a graph showing the curve $y = (x-1)(x-2)$ with a minimum at $x = \frac{3}{2}$, concave down for $x < \frac{3}{2}$, and concave up for $x > \frac{3}{2}$].

--- Page 61 ---

## EXAMPLE 2
### Sketch the curve $y=(x-1)(x-2)(x-3)$.

## SOLUTION
Here; $y=(x-1)(x-2)(x-3)$

* Put $y=0$ $x=1, 2, 3$.
* $y=x^3-6x^2+11x-6$

When $\frac{dy}{dx}=0$ 
$\frac{dy}{dx}=3x^2-12x+11$ and $\frac{d^2y}{dx^2}=6x-12$

* maximum when; $x=\frac{6-\sqrt{3}}{3}$ as $\frac{d^2y}{dx^2}=-2\sqrt{3}$
* minimum when; $x=\frac{6+\sqrt{3}}{3}$ as $\frac{d^2y}{dx^2}=2\sqrt{3}$

### (iii) Here; $\frac{dy}{dx}=3x^2-12x+11$

$= 3 \left(x - \frac{6-\sqrt{3}}{3}\right) \left(x - \frac{6+\sqrt{3}}{3}\right)$

Increases when; $x<\frac{6-\sqrt{3}}{3}$ or $x>\frac{6+\sqrt{3}}{3}$

Decreases when; $\frac{6-\sqrt{3}}{3}<x<\frac{6+\sqrt{3}}{3}$

### (iv) Concave upwards when $x>\frac{3}{2}$ or $x<\frac{3}{2}$.

[ A graph showing a cubic curve with x-intercepts at (1,0), (2,0), and (3,0), and a local maximum and minimum. The curve is concave up for $x>2$ and concave down for $x<2 $]

## EXAMPLE 3
### Sketch the curve $y=(x-1)^2(x-2)$.

## SOLUTION
Here; $y=(x-1)^2(x-2)$

* Put $y=0$ $x=1, 1, 2$
* $y=x^3-4x^2+5x-2$

When $\frac{dy}{dx}=0$ 
$\frac{dy}{dx}=3x^2-8x+5$ and $\frac{d^2y}{dx^2}=6x-8$

* maximum when; $x=1$ as $\frac{d^2y}{dx^2}=-2$.

--- Page 62 ---

### Curvature and Transformations

#### Example 4: Sketch the curve $y=3x^2-2x^3$.

##### Solution
The equation of the curve is $y=3x^2-2x^3$.

*   $x=0, \frac{3}{2}$ when $y=0$ 
*   $\frac{dy}{dx}=6x-6x^2=6x(1-x)$
*   $\frac{d^2y}{dx^2}=6-12x=6(1-2x)$

    *   Maximum when $x=1$ as $\frac{d^2y}{dx^2}=-6$
    *   Minimum when $x=0$ as $\frac{d^2y}{dx^2}=6$

    $y$ increases when $0<x<1$.

    $y$ decreases when $x<0$ and $x>1$.

    Concave up when $x<\frac{1}{2}$.

    Concave down when $x>\frac{1}{2}$.

[The graph of $y=3x^2-2x^3$ is a cubic curve with a maximum at $x=1$, a minimum at $x=0$, and an inflection point at $x=\frac{1}{2}$. The curve is concave up to the left of $x=\frac{1}{2}$ and concave down to the right.]

#### Example 5: Sketch the graph for the function $f(x)=|x+3|(x+1)$.

##### Solution
The equation of the curve is $y=|x+3|(x+1)$.

*   $y=(x+3)(x+1);\; x\geq -3$
*   $y=-(x+3)(x+1);\; x<-3$

    $x=-1,-3$ when $y=0$

*   $\frac{dy}{dx}= \begin{cases} 2x+4; & x\geq -3 \\ -2x-4; & x<-3 \end{cases}$

*   $\frac{d^2y}{dx^2}= \begin{cases} 2; & x\geq -3 \\ -2; & x<-3 \end{cases}$

[The graph of $y=|x+3|(x+1)$ consists of two parts: a smooth curve for $x\geq -3$ and a smooth curve for $x<-3$. There is a sharp turn at $x=-3$.]

--- Page 63 ---

## Step 1: Analyze the first graph
The first graph [a curve that increases from left to right with a maximum at $x = -3$, then decreases until $x = -2$ where it has a minimum, and then increases again, concave up when $x < -3$ and concave down when $x > -3$] shows the following properties:
- Increasing when $x < -3$ or $x > -2$
- Decreasing when $-3 < x < -2$
- Maximum at $x = -3$
- Minimum at $x = -2$
- Concave up when $x < -3$
- Concave down when $x > -3$

## Step 2: Analyze the function of Example 6
For the function $f(x) = \frac{x + 1}{x^2 + 3}$:
- $x = -1$ when $y = 0$
- $y = \frac{1}{3}$ when $x = 0$

## Step 3: Find the derivative of the function
The derivative $\frac{dy}{dx}$ is given by:
$\frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{-x^2 - 2x + 3}{(x^2 + 3)^2} = \frac{-(x + 3)(x - 1)}{(x^2 + 3)^2}$

## 4: Determine the intervals of increase and decrease
- Increasing when $-3 < x < 1$
- Decreasing when $x < -3$ or $x > 1$

## 5: Analyze the second graph
The second graph [a curve that starts from the bottom left, decreases until $x = -3$ where it has a minimum, then increases until $x = 1$ where it has a maximum, and then decreases again] shows the function $f(x) = \frac{x + 1}{x^2 + 3}$.

## Play with Graphs
### Functions and Graphs Description

## Function Properties
The function has the following properties:
- $x = -1$ when $y = 0$
- $y = \frac{1}{3}$ when $x = 0$

## Derivative
The derivative of the function $f(x) = \frac{x + 1}{x^2 + 3}$ is given by:
$\frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{-x^2 - 2x + 3}{(x^2 + 3)^2} = \frac{-(x + 3)(x - 1)}{(x^2 + 3)^2}$

## Intervals of Increase and Decrease
- Increasing when $-3 < x < 1$
- Decreasing when $x < -3$ or $x > 1$

## Graphical Transformations

### Using Number Line Rule

To analyze the function, we use the number line rule for $\frac{dy}{dx}$:

$\frac{d^2y}{dx^2} = \frac{2(x^3+3x^2-9x-3)}{(x^2+3)^3}$

The number line rule is represented as: [A number line with intervals marked as dec and inc, indicating where the function is decreasing or increasing].

From the given information:

* Minimum at $x = -3$ as $\frac{d^2y}{dx^2} = +\frac{1}{36} > 0$
* Maximum at $x = 1$ as $\frac{d^2y}{dx^2} = -\frac{1}{4} < 0$

### Note
In the above curve, the x-axis works as an asymptote, i.e., the curve would never meet the x-axis.

### Graphical Transformations

Here, we shall discuss the transformations as:

(i) $f(x)$ transforms to $f(x) \pm a$

(ii) $f(x)$ transforms to $f(x \pm a)$

(iii) $f(x)$ transforms to $(a f(x))$

(iv) $f(x)$ transforms to $f(ax)$

(v) $f(x)$ transforms to $f(-x)$

(vi) $f(x)$ transforms to $-f(x)$

(vii) $f(x)$ transforms to $-f(-x)$

(viii) $f(x)$ transforms to $|f(x)|$

(ix) $f(x)$ transforms to $f(|x|)$

(x) $f(x)$ transforms to $|f(|x|)|$

(xi) $y = f(x)$ transforms to $|y| = |f(x)|$

(xii) $y = f(x)$ transforms to $|y| = |f(|x|)|$

(xiii) $y = f(x)$ transforms to $y = [f(x)]$

(xiv) $y = f(x)$ transforms to $y = f([x])$

(xvi) $y = f(x)$ transforms to $y = [f([x])]$

(xvii) $y = f(x)$ transforms to $[y] = f(x)$

(xviii) $y = f(x)$ transforms to $[y] = [f(x)]$

(xix) $y = f(x)$ transforms to $y = f(\{x\})$

(xx) $y = f(x)$ transforms to $y = \{f(x)\}$

(xxi) $y = f(x)$ transforms to $y = \{f(\{x\})\}$

(xxii) $y = f(x)$ transforms to $\{y\} = f(x)$

(xxiii) $y = f(x)$ transforms to $\{y\} = \{f(x)\}$

(xxiv) $y = f(x)$ transforms to $y = f^{-1}(x)$, $f^{-1}(x)$ represents the inverse of $f(x)$.

## Transformation of Functions

### Vertical Shifts

When $f(x)$ transforms to $f(x) \pm a$ (where $a$ is positive), i.e.,
- $f(x)$ shifts to $f(x) + a$, the given graph of $f(x)$ shifts **upward** through '$a$' units.
- $f(x)$ shifts to $f(x) - a$, the given graph of $f(x)$ shifts **downward** through '$a$' units.

Graphically, this can be represented as: 
[ A graph showing three curves: $y=f(x)+a$, $y=f(x)$, and $y=f(x)-a$, with $a>0$ ]

## Solution

The graph of $y=e^x$ (an exponential function) is: 
[ A graph of the exponential function $y=e^x$ ]

The graph of $y=e^x + 1$ is shifted **upward** by 1 unit: 
[ A graph showing $y=e^x+1$ and $y=e^x$, with the upward shift labeled ]

The graph of $y=e^x - 1$ is shifted **downward** by 1 unit: 
[ A graph showing $y=e^x-1$ and $y=e^x$, with the downward shift labeled ]

\begin{cases}
-x; & x<0 \\
x; & x>0
\end{cases}
$$
[Graph of $y=|x|$ with a V-shape, opening upwards, symmetric about the y-axis, and intersecting the y-axis at (0,0)]

$y=|x|+2$ is shifted **upwards** by 2 units.

$$
$y=|x|-2$ is shifted **downwards** by 2 units.

## Transformations of Functions

### Vertical Shifts

The graph of $y = \sin^{-1} x + 1$ is shifted upwards by 1 unit.
The graph of $y = \sin^{-1} x - 1$ is shifted downwards by 1 unit.

[Figure: A graph showing $y = \sin^{-1} x$, $y = (\sin^{-1} x) + 1$, and $y = (\sin^{-1} x) - 1$ with the points $(1, \pi/2)$, $(1, \pi/2 + 1)$, and $(1, \pi/2 - 1)$ marked.]

### Horizontal Shifts

#### Theorem

* $f(x)$ transforms to $f(x - a)$; $a$ is positive. Shift the graph of $f(x)$ through '$a$' unit towards right.
* $f(x)$ transforms to $f(x + a)$; $a$ is positive. Shift the graph of $f(x)$ through '$a$' units towards left.

Graphically, it can be stated as:

[Figure: A graph showing $y = f(x)$, $y = f(x + a)$, and $y = f(x - a)$ with the points $(-a, 0)$, $(0, 0)$, and $(a, 0)$ marked.]

### Example

Plot $y=|x|$, $y=|x-2|$, and $y=|x+2|$.

## Solution

As discussed, $f(x) \rightarrow f(x - a)$; shift towards right.

$y = |x - 2|$ is shifted '2' units towards right.

The functions can be expressed as:

$y = |x| = 
\begin{cases}
-x; & x<0 \\
x; & x>0 
\end{cases}
$

$y=|x-2|=
\begin{cases}
-x+2; & x<2 \\
x-2; & x>2 
\end{cases}
$

[Figure: A graph showing $y = |x|$, $y = |x - 2|$, and $y = |x + 2|$.]

### Transformation of Functions

#### Vertical Stretch and Shrink

The transformation $f(x)$ to $af(x)$ can be described as follows:

*   **Stretch**: If $a > 1$, the graph of $f(x)$ is stretched 'a' times along the $y$-axis, i.e., $y = af(x)$.
*   **Shrink**: If $0 < a < 1$, the graph of $f(x)$ is shrunk '$\frac{1}{a}$' times along the $y$-axis, i.e., $y = \frac{1}{a}f(x)$.

[ A graph showing $y=f(x)$, $y=af(x)$, and $y=\frac{1}{a}f(x)$ with $a>1$ ]

### Example 1: Plotting Linear Functions

Plot $y = x$, $y = 2x$, and $y = \frac{1}{2} x$.

#### Solution

*   $y = 2x$ is a stretch of $f(x) = x$ '2' times along the $y$-axis.
*   $y = \frac{1}{2} x$ is a shrink of $f(x) = x$ '2' times along the $y$-axis.

[ A graph showing $y=x$, $y=2x$, and $y=\frac{1}{2}x$ ]

### Example 2: Plotting Trigonometric Functions

Plot $y = \sin x$ and $y = 2\sin x$.

#### Solution

The transformation $y = 2\sin x$ is a stretch of $y = \sin x$ '2' times along the $y$-axis.

[ A graph showing $y=\sin x$ and $y=2\sin x$ over the interval $[-\pi, \pi] $]

## Transformations of Functions

### Example 1: Vertical Shrinkage

Plot $y = \sin x$ and $y = \frac{1}{2} \sin x$.

#### Solution
As we know,
$y = \frac{1}{a} f(x)$

shrink the graph of $f(x)$ '$a$' times along $y$-axis.

$$
y = \frac{1}{2} \sin x
$$

shrink the graph of $f(x)$ '2' times along $y$-axis.

[The graph shows $y = \sin x$ (black) and $y = \frac{1}{2} \sin x$ (pink), with the latter being a vertical shrinkage of the former by a factor of $\frac{1}{2}$.]

### General Transformation Rules

#### Vertical Transformations
- $y = \frac{1}{a} f(x)$: Shrink (or contract) the graph of $f(x)$ '$a$' times along $y$-axis.

#### Horizontal Transformations
- $f(x)$ transforms to $f(ax)$ 
    - i.e., $f(x) \rightarrow f(ax); a > 1$
    - Shrink (or contract) the graph of $f(x)$ '$a$' times along $x$-axis.
- $f(x)$ transforms to $f(\frac{1}{a} x); a > 1$
    - Stretch (or expand) the graph of $f(x)$ '$a$' times along $x$-axis.

[The graph illustrates $y = f(ax)$, $y = f(x)$, and $y = f(\frac{1}{a}x)$, demonstrating the effects of horizontal transformations.]

### Example 2: Horizontal Shrinkage

Plot $y = \sin x$ and $y = \sin 2x$.

#### Solution
Here, $y = \sin 2x$ is to **shrink (or contract)** the graph of $\sin x$ by '2' units along $x$-axis. 

[The graph displays $y = \sin x$ (black) and $y = \sin 2x$ (pink), showing that $y = \sin 2x$ is a horizontal shrinkage of $y = \sin x$ by a factor of $\frac{1}{2}$.]

From the above figure, $\sin x$ is periodic with period $2\pi$ and $\sin 2x$ with period $\pi$.

To plot $y = \sin^{-1} \frac{x}{3} - 1$. We should follow as;

(ii) Plot $y = \sin^{-1} \frac{x}{3}$, i.e., stretch graph '3' units along $x$-axis.

(iii) Plot $y = \sin^{-1} \frac{x}{3} - 1$, i.e., shift the graph (ii) by '1' unit towards down.

## Transformations of Graphs

### Reflection Across Axes

When reflecting a graph across the $y$-axis and $x$-axis, the following transformations occur:

*   Reflection about the $y$-axis: $y = f(-x)$
*   Reflection about the $x$-axis: $y = -f(x)$

Combining these transformations:
*   $y = -f(-x)$

[Two graphs showing the transformations across the $y$-axis and $x$-axis, with $y=f(x)$ in black, $y=f(-x)$ in gray, $y=-f(x)$ in pink, and $y=-f(-x)$ in dark pink]

## Example 1: Plotting $y = -\mathrm{e}^{-x}$

### Solution

Given $y = \mathrm{e}^{x}$:

1.  Reflect $y = \mathrm{e}^{x}$ about the $y$-axis to obtain $y = \mathrm{e}^{-x}$.
2.  Reflect $y = \mathrm{e}^{-x}$ about the $x$-axis to obtain $y = -\mathrm{e}^{-x}$.

[Graph showing $y=e^x$ in black, $y=e^{-x}$ in gray, and $y=-e^{-x}$ in pink]

## Example 2: Plotting $y = -\log(-x)$

### Solution

Given $y = \log x$:

1.  Reflect $y = \log x$ about the $y$-axis to obtain $y = \log(-x)$.
2.  Reflect $y = \log(-x)$ about the $x$-axis to obtain $y = -\log(-x)$.

[Graph showing $y=\log x$ in black, $y=\log(-x)$ in gray, and $y=-\log(-x)$ in pink]

## Mathematical Transformation of Functions
The transformation from $f(x)$ to $|f(x)|$ involves reflecting the portion of $f(x)$ that lies below the $x$-axis about the $x$-axis.

### Transformation Steps
- Leave the positive part of $f(x)$ as it is.
- Reflect the negative part of $f(x)$ about the $x$-axis.

### Graphical Representation
- **Graph of $f(x)$**: [a curve that crosses the x-axis at several points, with parts above and below the x-axis].
- **Graph of $|f(x)|$**: [the same curve but with all parts below the x-axis reflected to be above the x-axis].

### Example Graph
- $y = -[-x]$ [a step function with specific points marked, reflecting about the y-axis].

This process can be represented by the equation:
$$
y = |f(x)|
$$

## Transformations of Graphs and Differentiability

### Note
Above transformation of graph is very important as to discuss differentiability of $f(x)$. As from above example we could say $y = f(x)$ is differentiable for all $x \in R - \{0\}$. But; $y = |f(x)|$ is differentiable for all $x \in R - \{-1, 0, 1\}$ as, "at sharp edges function is not differentiable."

### Example 1: Draw the graph for $y=|\log x|$

#### Solution
To draw graph for $y=|\log x|$ we have to follow two steps:

(i) Leave the (+ve) part of $y = \log x$, as it is

(ii) Take images of (-ve) part of $y = \log x$, i.e., the part below $x$-axis in the $x$-axis as plane mirror. Shown as:

[Graph of $y = \log x$: a curve that increases from negative infinity to positive infinity, with the $y$-axis as a vertical asymptote]

which is differentiable for all $x \in (0, \infty)$.

[Graph of $y=|\log x|$: a curve that increases from positive infinity to positive infinity, with the $y$-axis as a vertical asymptote, and a sharp edge at $x=1$]

which is clearly differentiable for all $x \in (0, \infty) - \{1\}$."as at $x = 1$ their is a sharp edge".

### Example 2: Draw the graph for $y=|x^2-2x-3|$

#### Solution
As we know the graph for $y=x^2-2x-3=(x-3)(x+1)$ is a parabola; so to sketch $y = |x^2 - 2x - 3|$ we have to follow two steps.

(i) Leave the positive part of $y = x^2 - 2x - 3$, as it is.

(ii) Take the image of negative part of $y = x^2 - 2x - 3$, i.e., the part below $x$-axis in the $x$-axis as plane mirror shown as in Fig. 

[Graph for $y = x^2 - 2x - 3 = (x-3)(x+1)$: a parabola with roots at $x=-1$ and $x=3$, and a minimum at $x=1$ and $y=-4$]

### Example 3: Sketch the graph for $y = |\sin x|$

#### Solution

To draw $y = |\sin x|$, we take the mirror image (in $x$-axis) of the portion of the graph of $\sin x$ which lies below $x$-axis.

### Transformations

#### $f(x)$ transforms to $f(|x|)$

If we know $y = f(x)$, then to plot $y = f(|x|)$, we should follow two steps:

1. Leave the graph lying right side of the $y$-axis as it is.
2. Take the image of $f(x)$ in the right of $y$-axis as the plane mirror and the graph of $f(x)$ lying leftward of the $y$-axis (if it exists) is omitted.

OR

Neglect the curve for $x < 0$ and take the images of curve for $x \geq 0$ about $y$-axis.

[Graph of $y = |x^2 - 2x - 3|$: a parabola with a vertex at $(1, -4)$ and $x$-intercepts at $(-1, 0)$ and $(3, 0)$, with all negative $y$ values reflected about the $x$-axis]

Clearly, the above curve is differentiable for all $x \in R - \{-1, 3\}$.

### Graph Descriptions
The following are descriptions of the graphs/figures shown:

* [A graph showing a curve $y=f(x)$ and its reflection about the $y$-axis, where the part of the curve for $x<0$ is neglected.]
* [A graph showing the curve $y=f(|x|)$.]
* [A graph showing the curve $y=\log x$ and its reflection about the $y$-axis, resulting in the curve $y=\log |x|$.]
* [A graph showing the curve $y=e^x$ and its reflection about the $y$-axis, resulting in the curve $y=e^{|x|}$.]

### Mathematical Equations and Text

To plot $y = e^{|x|}$, neglect the curve for $x < 0$ and take the image about the $y$-axis for $x \geq 0$. This can be achieved in two steps:
(i) Leave the graph lying on the right side of the $y$-axis as it is.
(ii) Take the image of $f(x)$ in the $y$-axis as a plane mirror.

## Example 1: Sketch the Curve $y = \log |x|$

### Solution
As we know, the curve $y = \log x$.

The equation can be drawn in two steps:

* Leave the graph lying on the right side of the $y$-axis as it is.
* Take the image of $f(x)$ in the $y$-axis as a plane mirror.

## Example 2: Plot the Curve $y = e^{|x|}$

### Solution
As we know the curve for $y = e^x$.

To plot $y = e^{|x|}$, neglect the curve for $x < 0$ and take the image about the $y$-axis for $x \geq 0$. 

The resulting curve $y = e^{|x|}$ can be represented by the equation:

$$ 
y = 
\begin{cases} 
e^x & \text{if } x \geq 0 \\
e^{-x} & \text{if } x < 0 
\end{cases}
$$ 

Similarly, for $y = \log |x|$, the equation can be represented as:

$$ 
y = 
\begin{cases} 
\log x & \text{if } x > 0 \\
\log (-x) & \text{if } x < 0 
\end{cases}
$$ 

or simply $y = \log |x|$.

(ii) $f(|x|)$ $\rightarrow$ $|f(|x|)|$

## Transformations of Functions

### Absolute Value Transformations

The following equations illustrate transformations involving absolute values:

*   $y = |f(x)|$ [A graph showing the transformation of $y=f(x)$ to $y=|f(x)|$, where the negative portions of $f(x)$ are reflected across the x-axis.]
*   $y = |f(|x|)|$ [A graph showing the transformation of $y=f(x)$ to $y=|f(|x|)|$, where the negative portions of $f(x)$ for $x<0$ are reflected across the y-axis and then the negative portions of $f(|x|)$ are reflected across the x-axis.]

## Example: Sketching Curves with Absolute Values

### Problem

Sketch the curve for $y = ||x|^2-2|x|-3|$.

### Solution

The graph for $y = x^2 - 2x - 3$ is:

[A graph of a parabola $y = x^2 - 2x - 3$ with roots at $(-1, 0)$ and $(3, 0)$ and vertex at $(1, -4)$.]

#### Step-by-Step Transformation

1.  **Original Function:** $y = x^2 - 2x - 3$
2.  **Replace $x$ with $|x|:$** $y = |x|^2 - 2|x| - 3$ [A graph showing $y = x^2 - 2x - 3$ and $y = |x|^2 - 2|x| - 3$, where the part of $y = x^2 - 2x - 3$ for $x<0$ is reflected across the y-axis.]
3.  **Take Absolute Value:** $y = ||x|^2 - 2|x| - 3|$ [A graph of $y = ||x|^2 - 2|x| - 3|$, where the negative portions of $y = |x|^2 - 2|x| - 3$ are reflected across the x-axis.]

The resulting graph $y = ||x|^2-2|x|-3|$ is differentiable for all $x \in \mathbb{R} - \{-3, 0, 3\}$.

## EXAMPLE 2 Sketch the graph for $y=\left| e^{-|x|} - \frac{1}{2} \right|$. 

### SOLUTION
As we know the graph for $y = e^{-x}$.

[The graph of $y = e^{-x}$ decreases exponentially from $y=1$ as $x$ increases from $-\infty$ to $\infty$.]

### Step-by-Step Transformations

#### (i) $y=e^{-x}$
[The graph of $y=e^{-x}$ is a decreasing exponential curve with $y$-intercept at $(0,1)$.]

#### (ii) $y=e^{-x}-\frac{1}{2}$ and $y=e^{-|x|}-\frac{1}{2}$
[The graph of $y=e^{-x}-\frac{1}{2}$ is the graph of $y=e^{-x}$ shifted down by $\frac{1}{2}$ units. The graph of $y=e^{-|x|}-\frac{1}{2}$ is the graph of $y=e^{-|x|}$ shifted down by $\frac{1}{2}$ units.]

#### (iii) $y=\left| e^{-|x|} - \frac{1}{2} \right|$
[The graph of $y=\left| e^{-|x|} - \frac{1}{2} \right|$ is the graph of $y=e^{-|x|}-\frac{1}{2}$ with the portion below the $x$-axis reflected above the $x$-axis.]

### Transformation Rule
(xi) $y = f(x)$ transforms to $|y| = f(x)$

Clearly $|y| \geq 0$ if $f(x) < 0$; graph of $|y| = f(x)$ would not exist.

if $f(x) \geq 0$; $|y| = f(x)$ would be given as $y = \pm f(x)$.

Hence, the graph of $|y| = f(x)$ exists only in the regions where $f(x)$ is non-negative and will be reflected about $x$-axis only when $f(x) \geq 0$. "Region where $f(x) < 0$ is neglected".

y = x^2 - 3x + 2
$$

$$
|y| = (x - 1)(x - 2) 
$$

### Example 2: Plot the Curve $|y| = \sin x$

#### Solution

The curve for $y = \sin x$ is given. Therefore, the graph for $|y| = \sin x$ can be obtained by:

*   Removing the portion of the graph below the $x$-axis.
*   Reflecting the removed portion across the $x$-axis.

The resulting graph will have the same shape as $y = \sin x$ but with all negative $y$ values reflected to be positive.

## Transformation of Functions
The function $y = f(x)$ transforms to $y = [f(x)]$, where $[ \infty ]$ denotes the greatest integer function.

## Steps to Draw $y = [f(x)]$
To draw $y = [f(x)]$, follow these steps:

1. Plot $f(x)$.
2. Mark the intervals of unit length with integers as end points on the $y$-axis.
3. Mark the corresponding intervals (with the help of the graph of $f(x)$) on the $x$-axis.
4. Plot the value of $[f(x)]$ for each of the marked intervals.

## Graphical Representation
The graphical representation of the transformation can be shown as: 
[Two graphs are shown. The first graph shows a continuous curve $y = f(x)$. The second graph shows a step-like curve $y = [f(x)]$ with horizontal lines and blank dots at the right end.]

## Example
### Sketch the Curve $y = [\sin x]$

## Solution
To sketch $y = [\sin x]$, first sketch $y = \sin x$. 
[The graph of $y = \sin x$ is a sinusoidal curve that oscillates between $-1$ and $1$.] 
The graph of $y = [\sin x]$ will be a step-like curve that takes on the greatest integer less than or equal to $\sin x$ at each point. 
Since $\sin x$ ranges from $-1$ to $1$, $[\sin x]$ will be $-1$ when $\sin x < 0$ and $0$ when $\sin x \geq 0$. 
[The graph of $y = [\sin x]$ is a step-like curve that oscillates between $-1$ and $0$.]

## Step 1: Plotting the Straight Lines
To plot the graph of $y = f([x])$, we first plot the straight lines parallel to the $y$-axis for integral values of $x$ (say $-3, -2, -1, 0, 1, 2, 3, ...$).

## Step 2: Marking Points on the Curve
Next, we mark the points at which $x = -3, x = -2, x = -1, x = 0, x = 1, ...$ on the curve.

## Step 3: Constructing the Required Curve
We take the lower marked point for $x$ say if $n < x < n + 1$ then take the point at $x = n$ and draw a horizontal line to the nearest vertical line formed by $x = n + 1$, proceeding in this way we get the required curve.

#### Section 1: $y = [e^{[x]}]$

The function $y = [e^{[x]}]$ can be described as follows:

- For $x < 0$, $0 < e^{[x]} < 1$, thus $y = 0$.
- For $0 \leq x < 1$, $e^{[x]} = 1$, thus $y = 1$.
- For $1 \leq x < 2$, $e^{[x]} = e$, thus $y = 2$.

Therefore, the function can be written as:
$$
y = 
\begin{cases}
0; & x < 0 \\
1; & 0 \leq x < 1 \\
2; & 1 \leq x < 2 \\
\end{cases}
$$

[Figure: A step graph with $y=0$ for $x<0$, $y=1$ for $0 \leq x < 1$, and $y=2$ for $1 \leq x < 2$]

When transforming $y = f(x)$ to $[y] = f(x)$, we only consider points where $f(x)$ is an integer, as $[y]$ represents integers for all $x$.

- **[Fig. 2.139] A graph of $y = f(\{x\})$, where $\{x\}$ denotes the fractional part of $x$, resulting in a periodic graph with a period of 1.**

### Equations

The equations mentioned in the text can be represented as:

- $y = [\sin x]$
- $[y] = [\sin x]$

### Text

The text describes the properties and transformations of various functions, including:

- The graph of $y = [\sin x]$ and its characteristics.
- The graph of $[y] = [\sin x]$ and its periodicity with a period of $2\pi$.
- The transformation of $y = f(x)$ to $y = f(\{x\})$ and the resulting graph.

The transformation rule for $f(x - [x])$ or $f(\{x\})$ is as follows:

- Graph of $f(x - [x])$ or $f(\{x\})$ can be obtained from the graph of $f(x)$ by following the rule: "Retain the graph of $f(x)$ for values of $x$ lying between interval $[0,1)$. Now it can be repeated for rest of the points. (taking periodicity 1)." 

The new obtained function is a graph for $y = f(\{x\})$. 

Graphically, it could be stated as:

- **Graph for $y = f(x)$**
- **Graph for $y = f(\{x\})$**, which is obtained by neglecting the parts of the graph outside the interval $[0,1)$.

### Sketching $h(x) =$ MAXIMUM $\{f(x), g (x)\}$ AND $h(x) =$ MINIMUM $\{f(x), g (x)\}$

#### (i) $h(x) =$ maximum$\{f(x), g (x)\}$

$$
h(x) =
\begin{cases}
f(x); & \text{when } f(x) > g(x) \\
g(x); & \text{when } g(x) > f(x)
\end{cases}
$$

Sketch $f(x)$ when its graph is above the graph of $g(x)$ and sketch $g(x)$ when its graph is above the graph of $f(x)$.

#### (ii) $h(x) =$ minimum$\{f(x), g (x)\}$

$$
h(x) =
\begin{cases}
f(x); & \text{when } f(x) < g(x) \\
g(x); & \text{when } g(x) < f(x)
\end{cases}
$$

Sketch $f(x)$ when its graph is lower and otherwise sketch $g(x)$.

## Step 1: Maximum and Minimum of Two Functions
The formulas to determine the maximum and minimum of two functions $f(x)$ and $g(x)$ are:
$\max\{f(x), g(x)\} = \frac{f(x) + g(x)}{2} + \left| \frac{f(x) - g(x)}{2} \right|$
$\min\{f(x), g(x)\} = \frac{f(x) + g(x)}{2} - \left| \frac{f(x) - g(x)}{2} \right|$

## Step 2: Graphing Functions of the Form $y = \max\{f(x), g(x)\}$ or $y = \min\{f(x), g(x)\}$
To draw the graph of functions of the form $y = \max\{f(x), g(x)\}$ or $y = \min\{f(x), g(x)\}$:
- First, draw the graphs of both functions $f(x)$ and $g(x)$ and their points of intersection.
- Then, find any two consecutive points of intersection. Between these points, if $f(x) > g(x)$, take those segments of $f(x)$ for $\max\{f(x), g(x)\}$.
- If $f(x) < g(x)$, take those segments of $g(x)$ for $\max\{f(x), g(x)\}$.
- Similarly, for $\min\{f(x), g(x)\}$, take those segments of $f(x)$ for which $f(x) < g(x)$, and segments of $g(x)$ for which $g(x) < f(x)$.

## Example 1: Sketch the Graph of $y = \max\{\sin x, \cos x\}$

### Solution
First, plot both $y = \sin x$ and $y = \cos x$ by a dotted curve as shown in [the graph in the interval $-\pi, \frac{3\pi}{2}$].
