## When $f(x), g(x) \rightarrow f(x) + g(x) = h(x)$

There is no direct approach; but we can use following steps if minimum or maximum value of any one is known.

### Steps:

1. Find maximum and minimum value of $g(x)$ say; a "$g(x)$".
2. Plot the curve $h(x) = f(x) + g(x)$ between $f(x) + a$ to $f(x) + b$. i.e.,
$f(x) + a \rightarrow h(x) \rightarrow f(x) + b$.
3. Check $g(x) = 0 \rightarrow h(x) = f(x)$.
4. When $g(x) > 0 \rightarrow h(x) > f(x)$.
5. When $g(x) < 0 \rightarrow h(x) < f(x)$.

## EXAMPLE 1
Sketch the curves of the following:

(i) $y = \sqrt{x - [x]}$  
(ii) $y = [x] + \sqrt{x - [x]}$  
(iii) $y = |[x] + \sqrt{x - [x]}|$  

## SOLUTION

### (i)
As we know that $0 < x - [x] < 1$ for all $x \in R$.  

Also, for any $x \in [0, 1)$, we have  
$\sqrt{x} < \sqrt{x - [x]}$  

Now, 
$y = \sqrt{x - [x]} = 
\begin{cases} 
\sqrt{x} & \text{when } 0 \leq x < 1 \\
\sqrt{x-1} & \text{when } 1 \leq x < 2 \\
\sqrt{x-2} & \text{when } 2 \leq x < 3 
\end{cases}$  
and so on.

Thus, from above we have to plot:  
$y = \sqrt{x}, \quad \text{when } 0 \leq x < 1$  
$y = \sqrt{x-1}, \quad \text{when } 1 \leq x < 2$  
$y = \sqrt{x-2}, \quad \text{when } 2 \leq x < 3$  

The curve for $y = \sqrt{x - [x]}$ is periodic with period $1$. [A graph of a periodic function with period 1, with the curve $y = \sqrt{x}$ from $0 \leq x < 1$, $y = \sqrt{x-1}$ from $1 \leq x < 2$, and $y = \sqrt{x-2}$ from $2 \leq x < 3$, and so on.]

### (ii)
As we know:  
$y = [x] + \sqrt{x - [x]}$  

So, 
$y = 
\begin{cases} 
-1 + \sqrt{x + 1} & \text{when } -1 \leq x < 0 \\
\sqrt{x} & \text{when } 0 \leq x < 1 \\
1 + \sqrt{x-1} & \text{when } 1 \leq x < 2 \\
2 + \sqrt{x-2} & \text{when } 2 \leq x < 3 
\end{cases}$  
and so on.

### EXAMPLE 6: Sketch the graph for:
(i) $y = e^{|x|} - e^{-x}$  
(ii) $y = |1 + e^{|x|} - e^{-x}|$  
(iii) $|y| = |1 + e^{|x|} - e^{-x}|$  
(iv) $|y|'' |1 + e^{|x|} - e^{-x}|$

## Step 3: Provide the solution
### SOLUTION

#### (i) Here $y = e^{|x|} - e^{-x}$

$$
y =
\begin{cases}
e^{x} - e^{-x} ; & x \geq 0 \\
e^{-x} - e^{-x} ; & x < 0
\end{cases}
$$

$$
y =
\begin{cases}
e^{x} - e^{-x} ; & x \geq 0 \\
0 ; & x < 0
\end{cases}
$$

$$
y = e^{x} - e^{-x}
$$

To discuss;

(i) when $x = 0$  $y = 0$(it passes through origin)

(ii) when $y = 0$  $e^{2x} - 1 = 0$  $x = 0$

(iii) $f(-x) = - f(x)$

$$
y = f(x) = e^{x} - e^{-x}
$$

$$
f(-x) = e^{-x} - e^{x} = - f(x);
$$

it shows $y = f(x) = e^{x} - e^{-x}$ is **odd function**, *i.e.*, symmetric about origin.

(iv) 

$$
\frac{dy}{dx} = e^{x} + e^{-x} = \frac{e^{2x} + 1}{e^{x}} > 0 \text{ for all } x \in R.
$$

$y$ is increasing for all $x$.

(v)

$$
\frac{d^{2}y}{dx^{2}} = e^{x} - e^{-x} = \frac{e^{2x} - 1}{e^{x}}
$$

$\frac{d^{2}y}{dx^{2}} > 0$  **when** $x > 0$; *concave up* and increasing .

$\frac{d^{2}y}{dx^{2}} < 0$  **when** $x < 0$; *concave down* and increasing from above discussion

$y = e^{x} - e^{-x}$; is plotted as shown in [a graph with an increasing curve that is concave down for $x<0$ and concave up for $x>0$, passing through the origin and increasing].

## Step 8: Sketch the curve

### (i) $|x+y|+|x-y| \lt 4$

#### SOLUTION

(i) As we know:

$$
\begin{aligned}
|x-y| &= 
\begin{cases}
|x|-|y|; & |x| \geq |y| \\
-(|x|-|y|); & |x| \lt |y|
\end{cases}
\end{aligned}
$$

Thus;

$$
\begin{aligned}
|x+y|+|x-y| &\lt 4 \\
x+y+x-y &\lt 4; & |x| &\geq |y| \\
x+y-x+y &\lt 4; & |x| &\lt |y| \\
2x &\lt 4; & |x| &\geq |y| \\
2y &\lt 4; & |x| &\lt |y| \\
\end{aligned}
$$

or 

$$
\begin{aligned}
|x+y|+|x-y| &\lt 4 \\
|x| &\lt 2 \quad \text{and} \quad |y| \lt 2 
\end{aligned}
$$

Thus, to shade the portion when $-2 \lt x \lt 2$ and $-2 \lt y \lt 2$. [Shown as a square region in the Cartesian plane bounded by the lines $x=-2, x=2, y=-2,$ and $y=2$, not including the boundary lines.]

### (ii) $|x+y|+|x-y| \geq 4$

Thus, to shade the portion when $x \leq -2$ or $x \geq 2$ and $y \leq -2$ or $y \geq 2$. [Shown as four separate regions in the Cartesian plane, each being a quadrant outside a square bounded by the lines $x=-2, x=2, y=-2,$ and $y=2$, including the boundary lines.]

## Example 10: Sketch the graph of the function
### $f(x) = \log_2 (1 - x^2)$

## Solution
Here, $\log_2 (1 - x^2)$ exists when $-1 < x < 1$

- Domain: $(-1, 1)$ 
- Range: $(-\infty, 0]$

As $\lim_{x \to \pm 1} y = -\infty$

$y = \log_2 (1 - x^2)$,

## Differentiating 
$\frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{(-2x)}{1 - x^2} (\log_2 e) = 2 \log_2 e \frac{-x}{(x^2 - 1)}$

## Using number line rule;
$\frac{dy}{dx} > 0$, when $-1 < x < 0$

$\frac{dy}{dx} < 0$, when $0 < x < 1$ 

## Also, 
$\frac{d^2y}{dx^2} = \frac{-2 \log_2 e(1 + x^2)}{(1 - x^2)^2} < 0$ for all $x \in (-1, 1)$

$\{y = \log(1 - x^2)$ is concave down for $x \in (-1, 1)\}$

## From above results we can draw
The graph of $y = \log_2 (1 - x^2)$ as shown in [a concave down graph in the interval $(-1, 1)$].

### Cleaned Markdown

### Section 1: Mathematical Analysis

The function $y = \frac{-1}{\log_{e} \sin x}$ is periodic with period $2\pi$.

To discuss the graph for $x \in (0, \pi) - \{\pi/2\}$ and sketch for the entire number line.

The derivative of the function is given by:

$$
\frac{dy}{dx} = +\frac{1}{(\log \sin x)^2} \cdot \frac{1}{\sin x} \cos x
$$

or

$$
\frac{dy}{dx} = +\frac{\cot x}{(\log \sin x)^2}
$$

The derivative is greater than zero when $x \in (0, \frac{\pi}{2})$ and less than zero when $x \in (\frac{\pi}{2}, \pi)$.

The second derivative is given by:

$$
\frac{d^2y}{dx^2} = \frac{(\log \sin x)^2 (-\csc^2 x) - \cot x \cdot 2(\log \sin x) \cot x}{(\log \sin x)^4}
$$

Simplifying, we get:

$$
\frac{d^2y}{dx^2} = -\frac{(\log \sin x)^2 \csc^2 x + 2(\log \sin x) \cot^2 x}{(\log \sin x)^4}
$$

Since $\frac{d^2y}{dx^2} > 0$ for all $x \in (0, \pi) - \{\pi/2\}$, the function is increasing and concave up on $x \in (\frac{\pi}{2}, \pi)$ and decreasing and concave up on $x \in (0, \frac{\pi}{2})$.

### Section 2: Graphs and Figures

### (i) Sketching of $[y] = \cos x$

From the above curve, $\cos x = -1, 0, 1$ when $x = -2\pi, -\frac{3\pi}{2}, -\pi, -\frac{\pi}{2}, 0, \frac{\pi}{2}, \pi, \frac{3\pi}{2}, 2\pi$

$[\cos x] = -1$ is possible only when $x = \pm 2\pi, \pm \frac{3\pi}{2}, \pm \pi, \pm \frac{\pi}{2}, 0$

Thus,
$$
\begin{aligned}
\cos x &= -1 & \implies -1" \; y<0 \\
\cos x &= 0  & \implies  0" \; y<0 \\
\cos x &= 1  & \implies  1" \; y<2 
\end{aligned}
$$

Thus, $[y]=\cos x$ is plotted as shown in [a graph of $y = \cos x$ with key points marked].

### (ii) Sketching of $[y] = [\cos x]$

First, plot $y = [\cos x]$ as shown in [a graph with points marked at $(-2\pi, 1)$, $(-\frac{3\pi}{2}, 0)$, $(-\pi, -1)$, $(-\frac{\pi}{2}, 0)$, $(0, 1)$, $(\frac{\pi}{2}, 0)$, $(\pi, -1)$, $(\frac{3\pi}{2}, 0)$, $(2\pi, 1)$].

From the above figure,
$$
\begin{aligned}
y &= -1 & \text{for } \frac{\pi}{2} < |x| < \frac{3\pi}{2} \\
y &= 0  & \text{for } 0 < |x| < \frac{\pi}{2} \text{ and } \frac{3\pi}{2} < |x| < 2\pi \\
y &= 1  & \text{for } |x| = 0, 2\pi 
\end{aligned}
$$

Thus, when $y = 0 \implies [y] \in [0, 1)$

when $y = 1 \implies [y] \in [1, 2)$

when $y = -1 \implies [y] \in [-1, 0)$

## Step 1: Analyze the given inequality
The given inequality is $\sin \frac{x+y}{2} \sin \frac{y-x}{2} > 0$. This inequality holds true for all points when $\sin \frac{x+y}{2}$ and $\sin \frac{y-x}{2}$ have the same sign.

## Step 2: Express the conditions for the inequality to hold
The inequality holds when:
- $\sin \frac{x+y}{2} > 0$ and $\sin \frac{y-x}{2} > 0$
- $\sin \frac{x+y}{2} < 0$ and $\sin \frac{y-x}{2} < 0$

## 3: Convert conditions into mathematical expressions
This implies:
- $x + y > 2n\pi$ and $y - x > 2m\pi$
- $x + y < 2n\pi$ and $y - x < 2m\pi$

or
- $y > 2n\pi - x$ and $y < 2m\pi + x$
- $y < 2n\pi - x$ and $y > 2m\pi + x$

## 4: Generalize the conditions
For general points and $n, m \in \mathbb{Z}$:
- $2k\pi < x + y < (2k+1)\pi$
- $2m\pi < y - x < (2m+1)\pi$

The equation $x + y = 2k\pi$ represents a system of parallel straight lines corresponding to different values of $k$.

## 6: Provide specific examples

- $y = -x$
- $y = -x + 2\pi$

### Description of the Graph

The graph for $x^{2/3} + y^{2/3} = a^{2/3}$ is an astroid curve. [The graph is a star-shaped curve with intercepts at $(a, 0)$, $(0, a)$, $(-a, 0)$, and $(0, -a)$, and is symmetric about both the x-axis and y-axis. The curve is concave up in the first quadrant and has a similar shape in the other three quadrants.]

### Mathematical Equations

The equation of the curve is given by:

$$x^{2/3} + y^{2/3} = a^{2/3}$$

The curve can also be represented parametrically as:

$$x = a \cos^3 t$$
$$y = a \sin^3 t$$

where $0 \leq t \leq 2\pi$ and $a > 0$.

### Properties of the Curve

The curve has the following properties:

*   It is symmetric about the y-axis, as replacing $x$ with $-x$ leaves the equation unchanged.
*   It is symmetric about the x-axis, as replacing $y$ with $-y$ leaves the equation unchanged.
*   It is symmetric about the origin, as replacing $x$ with $y$ and $y$ with $x$ leaves the equation unchanged.

### Derivatives

The first derivative of the curve is given by:

$$\frac{dy}{dx} = -\frac{y^{1/3}}{x^{1/3}}$$

The second derivative of the curve is given by:

$$\frac{d^2y}{dx^2} = \frac{1}{3} x^{-2/3} (a^{2/3} - x^{2/3}) \{4 + x^{-2/3} (a^{2/3} - x^{2/3})\}$$

The curve is symmetric about the y-axis, x-axis, and origin.

## Curvature and Transformations

### Sketching $y = \frac{e^x + e^{-x}}{2}$

#### Properties:
- When $x = 0$, $y = 1$
- $\frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{e^x - e^{-x}}{2} = \frac{e^{2x} - 1}{2e^x}$
- $\frac{d^2y}{dx^2} = \frac{e^x + e^{-x}}{2} = \frac{e^{2x} + 1}{2e^x} > 0$ for all $x$

From the above, 
- $\frac{dy}{dx} > 0$ whenever $x > 0$
- $\frac{dy}{dx} < 0$ whenever $x < 0$
- $\frac{d^2y}{dx^2} > 0$ for all $x$

Thus, from the properties, the graph of $y = \frac{e^x + e^{-x}}{2}$ is: 
[An increasing curve, concave up for $x > 0$, and decreasing, concave up for $x < 0$, with a minimum at $(0,1)$]

### Sketching $y = \frac{e^x - e^{-x}}{2}$

#### Properties:
- $f(-x) = -f(x)$, odd function or symmetric about the origin
- At $x = 0$, $y = 0$
- $\frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{e^x + e^{-x}}{2} > 0$ for all $x$
- $\frac{d^2y}{dx^2} = \frac{e^x - e^{-x}}{2} > 0$ when $x > 0$ and $\frac{d^2y}{dx^2} < 0$ when $x < 0$

From the properties, the graph of $y = \frac{e^x - e^{-x}}{2}$ is: 
[An increasing curve, concave up for $x > 0$, and increasing, concave down for $x < 0$, passing through the origin]

### Sketching $y = \frac{e^x - e^{-x}}{e^x + e^{-x}}$

#### Properties:
- $f(-x) = -f(x)$, odd function or symmetric about the origin
- At $x = 0$, $y = 0$
- Domain: $\mathbb{R}$
- Range: $(-1, 1)$
- $\frac{dy}{dx} > 0$ for all $x \in \mathbb{R}$

## Hyperbolic Functions

The hyperbolic functions are defined as:

$$
\begin{aligned}
\sin hx &= \frac{e^x - e^{-x}}{2} \\
\cos hx &= \frac{e^x + e^{-x}}{2} \\
\tan hx &= \frac{e^x - e^{-x}}{e^x + e^{-x}} \\
\cot hx &= \frac{e^x + e^{-x}}{e^x - e^{-x}} \\
\end{aligned}
$$

These functions satisfy the equation:

$$
x^2 - y^2 = \cos^2 ht - \sin^2 ht = 1
$$

which is the equation of a hyperbola.

### EXAMPLE 18: Sketch the curve $y=2 \sin x + \cos 2x$

#### SOLUTION
Since the function is periodic with period $2 \pi$, it is sufficient to investigate the function in the interval $[0, 2 \pi]$.

(a)
$\frac{dy}{dx} = 2 \cos x - 2 \sin 2x = 2(\cos x - 2 \sin x \cos x)$

$\frac{dy}{dx} = 2 \cos x (1 - 2 \sin x)$

Here, $\frac{dy}{dx} = 0$ at $x = \frac{\pi}{6}, \frac{\pi}{2}, \frac{5 \pi}{6}, \frac{3 \pi}{2}$

(b)
$\frac{d^2 y}{dx^2} = -2 \sin x - 4 \cos 2x$

$\left. \frac{d^2 y}{dx^2} \right|_{x = \frac{\pi}{6}} = -3 < 0$ where $y = \frac{3}{2}$ at $x = \frac{\pi}{6}$ is maximum.

$\left. \frac{d^2 y}{dx^2} \right|_{x = \frac{\pi}{2}} = 2 > 0$ at $x = \frac{\pi}{2}$ is minimum at $x = \frac{\pi}{2}$, where $y = 1$

at $x = \frac{5 \pi}{6}$ we have, $\left. \frac{d^2 y}{dx^2} \right|_{x = \frac{5 \pi}{6}} = -3 < 0$

and $y = \frac{3}{2}$ (maximum)

at $x = \frac{3 \pi}{2}$, we have, $\left. \frac{d^2 y}{dx^2} \right|_{x = \frac{3 \pi}{2}} = 6 > 0$

and $y = -3$ (minimum)

Thus, curve for $y=2 \sin x + \cos 2x$; 

[The graph of $y=2 \sin x + \cos 2x$ is a sinusoidal curve with amplitude 3/2 and  -3, and period $2 \pi$. The curve has a maximum at $(\frac{\pi}{6}, \frac{3}{2})$ and $(\frac{5 \pi}{6}, \frac{3}{2})$, and a minimum at $(\frac{\pi}{2}, 1)$ and $(\frac{3 \pi}{2}, -3)$.]

$\frac{dy}{dx} < 0$ for $x > 0$

$\frac{dy}{dx} > 0$ for $x < 0$

Increasing and concave up when $x > 0$.

## Example 22: Sketch the Curve

### Solution
Here; $y = \pm x^2 \sqrt{1 - x^2}$.

Thus, to plot the curve for $y = x^2 \sqrt{1 - x^2}$ and take the image about the x-axis.

* Symmetric about $x$ and $y$-axis.
* Domain: $[-1, 1]$
* Range: $\left[-\frac{2}{3\sqrt{3}}, \frac{2}{3\sqrt{3}}\right]$.
* When 
  * $x = 0$ $y = 0$
  * $y = 0$ $x = 0, \pm 1$.
* $2y \frac{dy}{dx} = 4x^3 - 6x^5 = 2x^3(2 - 3x^2) = 2x^3(\sqrt{2} - \sqrt{3}x)(\sqrt{2} + \sqrt{3}x)$

$\frac{dy}{dx} > 0$ when $0 < x < \sqrt{\frac{2}{3}}$ and $y > 0$.

$\frac{dy}{dx} < 0$ when $\sqrt{\frac{2}{3}} < x < 1$ and $y > 0$.

Here, we are sketching the curve only when $x, y \geq 0$ and then take the image about $x$ and $y$-axis.

* $\frac{d^2y}{dx^2} > 0$ when $0 < x < \sqrt{\frac{2}{5}}$ and $y > 0$.

$\frac{d^2y}{dx^2} < 0$ when $\sqrt{\frac{2}{5}} < x < 1$ and $y > 0$.

## Note
In the above curve $y^2 = x^3$ (semicubical parabola). For $x = 0$ we have $y = 0$ and $y = 0$, thus, the branch of the curve has a tangent at $y = 0$ at the origin. The second branch of $y = -\sqrt{x^3}$ also passes through the origin and has the same tangent $y = 0$. Thus, two different branches of the curve meet at the origin, have the same tangent, and situated at different sides of the origin. This is known as **cusp of first kind**.

## Note
At the origin (as the singular point) the two branches of the curve corresponding to plus and minus in front of the radical sign are mutually tangent. Known as **point of osculation** or **tacnode** or **double cusp**.

### Local Extrema

The following conditions describe local extrema:

*   (ii) If $f$ changes from negative to positive at $c$ ($f < 0$ for $x < c$ and $f > 0$ for $x > c$), then $f$ has a **local minimum** at $x = c$.

    [A graph showing a local minimum, where the function changes from negative to positive at $x=c$. In (a), $f(c) = 0$; in (b), $f(c)$ is undefined.]

*   (iii) If $f$ does not change sign at $c$ ($f$ has the same sign on both sides of $c$), then $f$ has **no local extreme value** at $c$.

    [A graph showing no local extreme value, where the function does not change sign at $x=c$. In (a), $f(c) = 0$; in (b), $f(c)$ is undefined.]

*   (iv) At a left end point **$a$**: If $f < 0$ ($f > 0$) for $x < a$, then $f$ has **local maximum** (**local minimum**) value at $x = a$.

    [A graph showing a local maximum and a local minimum at the left endpoint $x=a$.]

*   (v) At a right end point **$b$**: If $f < 0$ ($f > 0$) for $x > b$, then $f$ has **local minimum** (**local maximum**) at $x = b$.

    [A graph showing a local minimum and a local maximum at the right endpoint $x=b$.]

### Conditions for Local Maxima

The following conditions describe local maxima:

*   (i) If $f$ changes from positive to negative at $c$ ($f > 0$ for $x < c$ and $f < 0$ for $x > c$), then $f$ has a **local maximum** at $x = c$.

    [A graph showing a local maximum, where the function changes from positive to negative at $x=c$. In (a), $f(c) = 0$; in (b), $f(c)$ is undefined.]

## Asymptotes, Singular Points and Curve Tracing

In this chapter we shall study:

* Plotting of Rational and Irrational functions.
* Intersection of Curve and Straight line at Infinity.

### 3.1 Asymptotes

A straight line, at a finite distance from origin, is said to be an asymptote of the curve $y = f(x)$, if the perpendicular distance of the point $P$ on the curve from the line tends to zero when $x$ or $y$ both tends to infinity.

OR

A straight line $A$ is called an asymptote to a curve, if the distance from the variable point $M$ of the curve to this straight line approaches zero as the point $M$ tends to infinity. Shown as: 
[Two graphs showing a curve and its asymptote, with the curve approaching the asymptote as it tends to infinity]

### Mathematically

Let $y = f(x)$ be a curve and let $(x,y)$ be a point on it.

Tangent at $(x,y)$ is given by:

$$
\begin{aligned}
Y - y &= \frac{dy}{dx} (X - x) \\
Y &= \frac{dy}{dx} X + Y - x \frac{dy}{dx} 
\end{aligned}
$$
...(i)

--- Page 146 ---

## Asymptotes, Singular Points, and Curve Tracing

### Asymptote Exists

If an asymptote exists, then $x \circ \frac{dy}{dx}$ and $y - x \frac{dy}{dx}$ have a finite limit, say $m$ and $c$.

Say $\frac{dy}{dx} \to m$ and $y - x \frac{dy}{dx} \to c$.

Eq. (i) reduces to $Y = mX + c$ is an asymptote of the equation.

### Cases

* Asymptote parallel to $x$-axis
* Asymptote parallel to $y$-axis
* Asymptote of algebraic curves or oblique asymptotes
* Asymptote by inspection
* Intersection of curve and its Asymptotes
* Asymptote by Expansion
* The position of the curve with respect to asymptote

## 3.1 (i) Asymptote Parallel to $x$-axis

Let the equation of the curve be

$$
(a_0x^n + a_1x^{n-1}y + a_2x^{n-2}y^2 + \dots + a_ny^n) + (b_1x^{n-1} + b_2x^{n-2}y + \dots + b_ny^{n-1}) + (c_2x^{n-2} + c_3x^{n-3}y + \dots + c_ny^{n-2}) + \dots = 0
$$

then it can be arranged in descending powers of $x$ as follows:

$$
a_0x^n + (a_1y + b_1)x^{n-1} + (a_2y^2 + b_2y + c_2)x^{n-2} + \dots = 0
$$

Now, if $a_0 = 0$, i.e., the term consisting $x^n$ is absent, then $a_1y + b_1 = 0$, i.e., coefficient of $x^{n-1} = 0$ will make two roots of Eq. (i) infinite as coefficients of both $x^n$ and $x^{n-1}$ are zero.

Hence, $a_1y + b_1 = 0$ is an asymptote parallel to $x$-axis.

Again if; both $x^n$ and $x^{n-1}$ are absent, then $a_2y^2 + b_2y + c_2 = 0$, i.e., coefficient of $x^{n-2}$ being zero will make three roots of Eq. (ii) infinite hence, $a_2y^2 + b_2y + c_2 = 0$ **will give two asymptotes parallel to $x$-axis**.

### Method to Find Asymptote Parallel to $x$-axis

To find the asymptote parallel to $x$-axis equate the coefficient of highest power of $x$ to zero. If the coefficient is constant, then there is no asymptote parallel to $x$-axis (horizontal).

## 3.1 (ii) Asymptote Parallel to $y$-axis

From the above article, if we need an asymptote parallel to $y$-axis, equate the coefficient of highest power of $y$ to zero. If this coefficient is constant, then there is no asymptote parallel to $y$-axis (vertical).

$y = 0$ (equating highest power of $x = 0$)

From adjoining figure [a graph of $y = e^{1/x}$ with an asymptote $y=1$ and $x=0$]

y = e^{1/x}
$$

$$
\frac{1}{x} = \log y
$$

$$
x = \frac{1}{\log y}
$$

which shows $x(\log y) = 1$ has an asymptote parallel to $x$-axis as

$$
\log y = 0 \quad y = 1.
$$

Thus, $y = e^{1/x}$ has two asymptotes

$x = 0$ and $y = 1$.

### 3.1 (iii) Asymptote of algebraic curves or oblique asymptote

An asymptote which is not parallel to $y$-axis is called an oblique asymptote. Let $y = mx + c$ be an asymptote of $y = f(x)$, then

$$
m = \lim_{x \to \infty} \frac{y}{x} \quad \text{and} \quad c = \lim_{x \to \infty} (y-mx)
$$

### Method to find oblique asymptote

Suppose $y = mx + c$ is an asymptote of the curve. Put $y = mx + c$ in the equation of the curve and arrange it in descending powers of $x$. Equate to zero the coefficients of two highest degree terms. Solve these two equations, find $m$ and $c$. Put them in $y = mx + c$ to get asymptotes.

> **Note**
> 1. Here, we will find non-parallel or non repeated asymptote only.
> 2. Neglect all imaginary values of $m$.

### Example 2

Find the asymptotes of the curve $y = \frac{x^2 + 2x - 1}{x}$ and hence, sketch.

## Solution

Here, the curve $y = \frac{x^2 + 2x - 1}{x}$ could be written as;

$$x^2 + 2x - yx - 1 = 0$$ 

### Asymptotes

*   No asymptote parallel to $x$-axis.
*   Asymptote parallel to $y$-axis: $x = 0$.
*   Oblique asymptote

Let $y = mx + c$ be oblique asymptote

$$
\begin{aligned}
x^2 + 2x - x(mx + c) - 1 &= 0 \\
x^2 - mx^2 + 2x - cx - 1 &= 0 \\
x^2(1 - m) + x(2 - c) - 1 &= 0
\end{aligned}
$$

For oblique asymptote equate highest power and second highest power of $x$ to zero.

i.e.,

$$
\begin{aligned}
\text{Coefficient of } x^2 &= 0 & m &= 1 \\
\text{Coefficient of } x &= 0 & c &= 2
\end{aligned}
$$

$y = x + 2$ is oblique asymptote to $y = x - \frac{1}{x} + 2$.

## Asymptotes, Singular Points and Curve Tracing

### Example 1: Find the asymptote of the curve $x^2y + xy^2 = a^3$.

#### Solution
The given curve is $x^2y + xy^2 = a^3$ or $x^2y + xy^2 - a^3 = 0$.

This equation is of the form $F_n + F_{n-2} = 0$ 

Here, 
$F_3 = x^2y + xy^2$ and $F_0 = -a^3$.

By inspection the asymptotes are given by 
$x^2y + xy^2 = 0$ or $xy(x+y) = 0$.

The asymptotes are $x=0$, $y=0$, $x+y=0$.

[Figure of a graph with asymptotes x=0, y=0, and x+y=0]

### Example 2: Find the asymptote of the curve $y = x + \frac{1}{x}$ (by Inspection).

#### Solution
The given curve is $x^2 - xy + 1 = 0$.

This equation is of the form $F_n + F_{n-2} = 0$

Here 
$F_2 = x^2 - xy$ 
$F_0 = 1$

By inspection the asymptotes are given by 
$x^2 - xy = 0$ or $x(x-y) = 0$.

The asymptotes are $x=0$ and $x-y=0$.

### 3.1 (v) Intersection of curve and its asymptote

An asymptote of curve of $n$th degree cut the curve in $(n - 2)$ points provided the asymptote is not parallel to any asymptote.

Hence, if there be $N$ asymptotes of the curve, then they cut the curve in $N(n - 2)$ points.

**Note**: The number of asymptotes of an algebraic curve of $n$th degree can not be more than $n$.

### Example 1: Show the asymptote of the curve $xy(x^2 - y^2) + x^2 + y^2 - 1 = 0$ cut at 8 points.

#### Solution
The equation of the curve is,
$xy(x^2 - y^2) + x^2 + y^2 - 1 = 0$ ...(i)

Here $n=4$

This equation is of the type $F_n + F_{n-2} = 0$

Hence, 
$F_n = xy(x^2 - y^2) = xy(x - y)(x + y)$

and 
$F_{n-2} = x^2 + y^2 - 1$

$F_n = 0$

$x = 0$, $y = 0$, $x - y = 0$ and $x + y = 0$ are the equations of asymptotes.

The combined equation of the asymptotes is,
$xy(x - y)(x + y) = 0$ ...(ii)

[Figure of a graph with asymptotes x=0, y=0, x-y=0, and x+y=0]

--- Page 153 ---

## 3.1 (vi) Asymptote by expansion

If the equation of the curve is of the form

$$ 
y=mx+c + \frac{A}{x} + \frac{B}{x^2} + \sup
$$

Then $y=mx+c$ will be an asymptote of the given curve.

### Example 1: Find the asymptote of the curve $y^3 = x^2(x - a)$.

#### Solution
The curve is 
$$
\begin{aligned}
y^3 &= x^2(x - a) \\
&= x^3 \left(1 - \frac{a}{x} \right) \\
y &= x \left(1 - \frac{a}{x} \right)^{1/3} \\
&= x \left[ 1 - \frac{1}{3} \frac{a}{x} - \frac{1}{9} \frac{a^2}{x^2} - \sup \right] \\
&= x - \frac{a}{3} - \frac{1}{9} \frac{a^2}{x} - \sup 
\end{aligned}
$$

which is of the form 
$$
y = mx + c + \frac{A}{x} + \frac{B}{x^2} + \sup
$$

is asymptote

Hence, $y = x - \frac{a}{3}$ is asymptote of the given curve.

### Example 2: Find the asymptote for $y = x + \frac{1}{x}$.

#### Solution
Here; $y = x + \frac{1}{x}$ is of the form,
$$
y=mx+c + \frac{A}{x} + \frac{B}{x^2} + \sup
$$

$y = x$ is asymptote of the curve 
$$
y = x + \frac{1}{x}.
$$

## 3.1 (vii) The position of the curve with respect to an asymptote

Let the equation of the curve is of the form;
$$
y=mx+c + \frac{A}{x} + \frac{B}{x^2} + \frac{C}{x^3} + \sup
$$

--- Page 154 ---

## Asymptotes, Singular Points and Curve Tracing

### Conditions for Curve and Asymptote Positioning

* The curve lies above the asymptote if:
  * (i) $A \uparrow 0$ and $A$ and $x$ have the same signs
  * (ii) $A = 0$, $B > 0$
  * (iii) $A = 0$, $B = 0$, $C \uparrow 0$ and $C$ and $x$ have the same signs

* The curve lies below the asymptote if:
  * (i) $A \uparrow 0$ and $A$ and $x$ have opposite signs
  * (ii) $A = 0$, $B < 0$
  * (iii) $A = 0$, $B = 0$, $C \uparrow 0$ and $C$ and $x$ have opposite signs

### Example 1

For the curve $y^5 = x^5 + 2x^4$, show:

* (i) The curve lies above the asymptote $y = x + \frac{2}{5}$ if $x < 0$
* (ii) The curve lies below the asymptote $y = x + \frac{2}{5}$ if $x > 0$

## Solution

The given curve is,
$$
y^5 = x^5 + 2x^4
$$
or 
$$
y^5 = x^5 \left(1 + \frac{2}{x}\right)
$$
$$
y = x \left(1 + \frac{2}{x}\right)^{1/5}
$$
The asymptote is 
$$
y = x \left(1 + \frac{2}{5} \frac{1}{x} - \frac{8}{25} \frac{1}{x^2} \right) = x + \frac{2}{5} - \frac{8}{25x} \cdots
$$
or 
$$
y = x + \frac{2}{5};
$$

* (i) Now if $A = -\frac{8}{25}$ and $x$ have the same sign $\quad x<0$. Then the curve lies above the asymptote.
* (ii) Now if $A = -\frac{8}{25}$ and $x$ have opposite signs $\quad x>0$. Then the curve lies below the asymptote.

### Example 2

For the curve $y = x + \frac{1}{x}$, show,

* (i) The curve lies above the asymptote $y = x$ if $x > 0$ [Figure of a hyperbola with asymptotes $y=x$ and $y=-x$ and curve $y = x + \frac{1}{x}$]
* (ii) The curve lies below the asymptote $y = x$ if $x < 0$

## Solution

The given curve is, $y = x + \frac{1}{x}$, is of the form 
$$
y = mx + c + \frac{A}{x} + \frac{B}{x^2} + \frac{C}{x^3} \cdots
$$

--- Page 155 ---

## Singular Points

Thus, $y=x$ is the asymptote to $y=x+\frac{1}{x}$.

(i) Now if $A=1$ and $x$ have same sign $x>0$, then the curve lies above the asymptote.

(ii) Now if $A=1$ and $x$ have opposite sign $x<0$, then the curve lies below the asymptote.

(i) Multiple points
(ii) Double points 
    Types of double points :
    (a) Node 
    (b) Cusp 
    (c) Isolated point
(iii) Tangent at the origin.

(v) Types of cusps.

### 3.2 (i) Multiple points

A point on a curve is said to be a multiple point of order $r$, if $r$ branches of the curve pass through this point.

If $P$ is the multiple point of order $r$, then there will be $r$ tangents at $P$, one of each of the $r$ branches. These $r$ tangents may be real, imaginary, distinct, coincident.

### 3.2 (ii) Double points

A point on a curve is said to be a double point of the curve, if two branches of the curve pass through this point.

Double points have two tangents, they may be real, imaginary, distinct or coincident.

Types of Double points

#### (a) Node

If the two branches of a curve pass through the double point and the tangents to them at the point are real and distinct, then the double point is called a **node** [a graph with two distinct curves intersecting at a point with two distinct tangents].

#### (b) Cusp

If the two branches of the curve pass through the double point and the tangent to them are the point is real and coincident, then the double point is called **cusp** [a graph with two curves intersecting at a point with a single tangent, forming a sharp point].

--- Page 156 ---

## Cusps

The graph of a continuous function $y = f(x)$ has a cusp at a point $x = c$ if the concavity is **same** on both sides of $c$ and either:

1. $\lim_{x \to c^-} f(x) = \infty$ and $\lim_{x \to c^+} f(x) = -\infty$
   **OR**
2. $\lim_{x \to c^-} f(x) = -\infty$ and $\lim_{x \to c^+} f(x) = \infty$

[Figure with a graph showing a cusp, where the curve approaches positive infinity from the left and negative infinity from the right, or vice versa.]

**Note**: A cusp can either be a local maximum (1) or a local minimum as in (2).

## Isolated Point

If there are no real points on the curve in the neighborhood of a point $P$ is called an **isolated** or a **conjugate point**.

[Figure with a graph showing an isolated point, where there are no real points on the curve in the neighborhood of the point.]

## Tangent at the Origin

If an algebraic curve passes through the origin, the equation of tangent or tangents at the origin is obtained by equating to zero the lowest degree terms in the equation of the curve.

## Example

Show that the curve $y^2 = 4x^2 + 9x^4$ has a node at the origin and hence, sketch.

## Solution

The equation of the curve is,

$$y^2 = 4x^2 + 9x^4$$

## 7: 3.2 (iv) Necessary conditions for the existence of double points
Let $(x,y)$ be a point on the given curve $f(x, y) = 0$.

The necessary and sufficient conditions for $(x,y)$ to be a double points are:
$f = 0$, $\frac{\partial f}{\partial x} = 0$, $\frac{\partial f}{\partial y} = 0$ at $(x, y)$

## Asymptotes, Singular Points and Curve Tracing

Now, if $\frac{\partial^2 f}{\partial x \partial y}$, $\frac{\partial^2 f}{\partial x^2}$, and $\frac{\partial^2 f}{\partial y^2}$ are not all zero, then,

(i) Double point will be a node if
$$
\left(\frac{\partial^2 f}{\partial x \partial y}\right)^2 - \frac{\partial^2 f}{\partial x^2} \frac{\partial^2 f}{\partial y^2} > 0
$$
or
$$
f_{xy}^2 - f_{xx} f_{yy} > 0
$$

(ii) The double point will be an isolated point, if
$$
f_{xy}^2 - f_{xx} f_{yy} < 0
$$

(iii) The double point will be a cusp if
$$
f_{xy}^2 - f_{xx} f_{yy} = 0.
$$
Here, if $f_{xx} = f_{xy} = f_{yy} = 0$ at $(x, y)$, then it will be a multiple point of order greater than 2.

### Example 1
For the curve $x^3 + x^2 + y^2 - x - 4y + 3 = 0$, find the double point and hence, whether the point is node or isolated point.

### Solution
Let $f(x, y) = x^3 + x^2 + y^2 - x - 4y + 3 = 0$

$$
\begin{aligned}
f_x &= 3x^2 + 2x - 1 \\
f_y &= 2y - 4 
\end{aligned}
$$
for a double point $f_x = 0$, $f_y = 0$

or
$$
\begin{aligned}
f_x &= 0 \quad 3x^2 + 2x - 1 = 0 \\
x &= \frac{1}{3}, -1 \\
f_y &= 0 \quad 2y - 4 = 0 \quad y = 2 
\end{aligned}
$$

Possible double points are $\frac{1}{3}, 2 , (-1, 2)$

$$
\begin{aligned}
f \frac{1}{3}, 2 &\uparrow 0 \\
f(-1, 2) &= 0 
\end{aligned}
$$

$f(-1, 2)$ **is a double point.**

$$
\begin{aligned}
f_{xx} &= 6x + 2 \quad f_{xx} \text{ at } (-1, 2) = -4 \\
f_{xy} &= 0 \quad f_{xy} \text{ at } (-1, 2) = 0 \\
f_{yy} &= 2 \quad f_{yy} \text{ at } (-1, 2) = 2 \\
f_{xy} - f_{xx} f_{yy} &= 0 - (-4)(2) = 8 > 0 
\end{aligned}
$$

$(-1, 2)$ **may be node.**

For shifting origin to $(-1, 2)$, substitute $x = X - 1$, $y = Y + 2$ in the given equation, we get,

$$
\begin{aligned}
(X - 1)^3 - 2(X - 1)^2 + (Y + 2)^2 &= 0 \\
X^3 - 2X^2 + Y^2 &= 0 \\
Y &= \pm X \sqrt{2 - X}
\end{aligned}
$$
For numerically small values of $X, Y$ is real.

$(-1, 2)$ **is a node on the given curve.** 

[No diagram or graph is present on this page.]

--- Page 159 ---

### EXAMPLE 2

For the curve $x^3 + 2x^2 + 2xy - y^2 + 5x - 2y = 0$, find the double point and hence, check whether node, cusp or isolated point.

### SOLUTION

Let 
$f(x, y) = x^3 + 2x^2 + 2xy - y^2 + 5x - 2y = 0$  ... (i)

$f_x = \frac{\partial f}{\partial x} = 3x^2 + 4x + 2y + 5$

$f_y = \frac{\partial f}{\partial y} = 2x - 2y - 2$

$f_{xx} = \frac{\partial^2 f}{\partial x^2} = 6x + 4$

$f_{xy} = \frac{\partial^2 f}{\partial x \partial y} = 2$

$f_{yy} = \frac{\partial^2 f}{\partial y^2} = -2$

For double points
$f_x = f_y = f = 0$

$f_x = 0 \implies 3x^2 + 4x + 2y + 5 = 0$  ... (ii)

$f_y = 0 \implies 2x - 2y - 2 = 0$  ... (iii)

Solving Eqs. (ii) and (iii), we get 
$3x^2 + 4x + 2x - 2 - 2 + 5 = 0$

$\implies x = -1$

$\implies 2y = 2x - 2$

$\implies 2y = 2(-1) - 2$

$\implies y = -2$

$\therefore x = -1, y = -2$ satisfies the given equation.

$(-1, -2)$ is a double point.

At $(-1, -2)$,

$f_{xx} = 6(-1) + 4 = -2$

$f_{xy} = 2, f_{yy} = -2$

$\therefore f_{xy}^2 - f_{xx} f_{yy} \text{ at } (-1,-2) = 0$

$\therefore (-1, -2)$ may be a cusp.

For shifting the origin to $(-1,-2)$ substitute $x=X-1, y=Y-2$ in the given equation.

$\implies (X-1)^3+2(X-1)^2+2(X-1)(Y-2)-(Y-2)^2+5(X-1)-2(Y-2) = 0$

$\implies X^3 - X^2 + 2XY - Y^2 = 0$  ... (iv)

$\implies Y = X \pm X \sqrt{X}$

$Y$ is real for all positive value of $X$.

Two branches of (iv) pass through origin.

Two branches of (i) pass through $(-1, -2)$.

$\therefore (-1,-2)$ is a cusp.

### EXAMPLE 3

Find for the curve $y^2 = x \sin x$ origin is node, cusp or isolated point.

### SOLUTION

Let 
$f(x, y) = y^2 - x \sin x$

$f_x = - \sin x - x \cos x$

$f_y = 2y$

$f_{xx} = - \cos x + x \sin x - \cos x$

### 3.2 (v) Types of Cusps

When two branches of a curve pass through a cusp and the tangents at cusp are coincident. Therefore, normal to the branches at a cusp would also be coincident.

Cusp can be of five kinds

#### (a) Single cusp

If the branches of the curve lie on the same side of the common normal, then the cusp is called a single cusp.

[Two graphs side-by-side showing a single cusp of the first species and a single cusp of the second species, with labels for tangent and normal lines]

#### (b) Double cusp

If the branches of the curve lie on the both sides of the common normal, then the cusp is called double cusp.

Here, both the branches of the curve lie on the both sides of common tangent, then the cusp is of first kind.

Also if, the branches of the curve lie on the same side of the common tangent, then the cusp is called **cusp of second species** or **Ramphoid cusp**.

[Two graphs side-by-side showing a double cusp of the first species and a ramphoid cusp, with labels for tangent and normal lines]

at $x=0$:
$f_{xx} = -2$, $f_{xy} = 0$, $f_{yy} = 2$

$f_{xy}^2 - f_{xx} f_{yy} \text{ at } (0, 0)$

$0 + 2(2) = 4 > 0$

$f_{xy}^2 - f_{xx} f_{yy} > 0$

The origin is node.

## EXAMPLE 1 Sketch the curve $y^2(a + x) = x^2(a - x)$.

## SOLUTION 
Here, the curve is 
$y^2(a + x) = x^2(a - x)$  ...(i)

1. The curve is symmetrical about $x$-axis.

2. Curve passes through origin and cuts the $x$-axis at a point $(a, 0)$.

3. Equating to zero the lowest degree terms of Eq. (i), we get the tangents at origin.
$y^2 = x^2$  or  $y = \pm x$

The curve has a node at the origin.

4. Equating to zero the highest degree term, we get the asymptote
$x + a = 0$,  i.e.,  $x = - a$.

$y = \pm x \sqrt{\frac{a-x}{a+x}}$

5. From Eq. (i)
$y$ exists when $-a < x < a$  Domain $(-a, a)$.

6. As $x$ increases from $0$ to $a$ $y$ increases up to a point then decreases to zero.
$y$ increases when $x \in [0, a]$
$y$ decreases when $x \in (-a, 0]$

Thus, $y^2(a + x) = x^2(a - x)$ could be plotted as; 
[ A graph with x and y axes, a node at the origin, and a curve that has a tangent at the origin and an asymptote at $x=-a$ and is symmetrical about the $x$-axis. The curve starts at $(-a,0)$ and ends at $(a,0)$ with $y$ increasing then decreasing as $x$ goes from $0$ to $a$. ]

## 3.3 Remember for Tracing Cartesian Equation

### 1. Check Symmetry
* A curve is symmetrical about $x$-axis, i.e., $y$ is replaced by $-y$ and curve remains same.
* A curve is symmetrical about $y$-axis, i.e., $\mathrm{f}(-\mathrm{x}) = \mathrm{f}(\mathrm{x})$.
* A curve is symmetrical about $y = x$, i.e., on interchanging $x$ and $y$ curve remains same. [Figure of symmetry about y = x]
* A curve is symmetrical about $y = -x$. i.e., on interchanging $x$ by $-y$ and $y$ by $-x$ curve remains same.
* The curve is symmetrical in opposite quadrants, i.e., $\mathrm{f}(-\mathrm{x}) = -\mathrm{f}(\mathrm{x})$.

### 2. Check for Origin
Find whether origin lies on the curve or not. If yes, check for multiple points (See Art. 3.2).

### 3. Point of Intersection with $x$-axis and $y$-axis
Put $x = 0$ and find $y$, put $y = 0$ and find $x$. Also obtain the tangents at such points.

### 4. Asymptotes
Find the point at which asymptote meets the curve and equation of asymptote (see Art. 3.1) [Figure of asymptotes]

### 5. Domain and Range
To check in which part the curve lies.

### 6. Monotonicity and Maxima Minima
Find $\frac{dy}{dx}$ and check the interval in which $y$ increases or decreases and the point at which it attains maximum or minimum.

### 7. Concavity and Convexity
The interval in which,
$\frac{d^2y}{dx^2} > 0$

and 
$\frac{d^2y}{dx^2} < 0$

Using all the above results we can sketch the curve 
$y = \mathrm{f}(\mathrm{x})$.

## Step 1: Analyze the given equation
The equation given is $y^2(a^2 + x^2) = x^2(a^2 - x^2)$. This can be rewritten as $y^2 = \frac{x^2(a^2 - x^2)}{(a^2 + x^2)}$.

## Step 2: Describe the curve
The curve is symmetric about the $x$-axis and $y$-axis. It passes through the origin, and $y = \pm x$ are two tangents at the origin. The curve meets the $x$-axis at $(a, 0)$, $(0, 0)$, and $(-a, 0)$ and meets the $y$-axis at $(0, 0)$ only. The tangents at $(a, 0)$ and $(-a, 0)$ are $x = a$ and $x = -a$ respectively. The curve has no asymptote.

## Step 3: Express $y$ in terms of $x$
$y = \pm x \sqrt{\frac{a^2 - x^2}{a^2 + x^2}}$.

## 4: Determine the domain
The domain of the curve is $[-a, a]$.

## 5: Find $\frac{dy}{dx}$
$\frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{a^4 - 2a^2x^2 - x^4}{(a^2 + x^2)^{3/2}(a^2 - x^2)^{1/2}}$ for $x \in [-a, a]$.

## 6: Analyze $\frac{dy}{dx} = 0$
$\frac{dy}{dx} = 0$ when $a^4 - 2a^2x^2 - x^4 = 0$. This can be factored as $\frac{dy}{dx} = -\frac{\{x^4 + 2a^2x^2 + a^4 - 2a^4\}}{(a^2 + x^2)^{3/2}(a^2 - x^2)^{1/2}} = -\frac{\{(x^2 + a^2)^2 - (\sqrt{2}a^2)^2\}}{(a^2 + x^2)^{3/2}(a^2 - x^2)^{1/2}} = -\frac{\{x - \sqrt{(-1 + \sqrt{2})a}\}\{x + \sqrt{(-1 + \sqrt{2})a}\} \{x^2 + (1 + \sqrt{2})a^2\}}{(a^2 + x^2)^{3/2}(a^2 - x^2)^{1/2}}$.

## 7: Determine critical points
$\frac{dy}{dx} = 0$ when $x = \pm \sqrt{(-1 + \sqrt{2})a}$.

## 8: Analyze the sign of $\frac{dy}{dx}$
$\frac{dy}{dx} = +ve$; $x \in (-\sqrt{(-1 + \sqrt{2})a}, \sqrt{(-1 + \sqrt{2})a})$ and $\frac{dy}{dx} = -ve$; $x \in (-a, -\sqrt{(-1 + \sqrt{2})a})$ or $(\sqrt{(-1 + \sqrt{2})a}, a)$.

## 9: Determine intervals of increase and decrease
$y$ is increasing when $x \in (-\sqrt{(-1 + \sqrt{2})a}, \sqrt{(-1 + \sqrt{2})a})$ and $y$ is decreasing when $x \in (-a, -\sqrt{(-1 + \sqrt{2})a})$ or $(\sqrt{(-1 + \sqrt{2})a}, a)$.

## Asymptotes, Singular Points and Curve Tracing

### Example 2: Sketch the curve $y^2(x - a) = x^2(a + x)$

## Solution

1. Symmetrical about $x$-axis only.

2. It passes through origin and $y^2 + x^2 = 0$, i.e., $y = \pm ix$ are two imaginary tangents at origin. Thus, origin is **isolated point**.

3. It meets $x$-axis at $(-a, 0), (0, 0)$ and $y$-axis at $(0, 0)$.

The tangent at $(-a, 0)$ is $x = -a$.

4. $y = \pm (x - a)$ and $x = a$ are three asymptotes.

5. 
$y^2 = \frac{x^2(x + a)}{(x - a)} \quad y = \pm x \sqrt{\frac{x + a}{x - a}}$

Thus, for domain;
$\frac{x+a}{x-a} \geq 0$ and $x \uparrow a$

i.e.,
$x < -a \quad \text{or} \quad x > a$

Domain $(-\infty, -a] \cup (a, \infty) \quad \{0\}$

6. 
$\frac{dy}{dx} = \pm \frac{x^2 - ax - a^2}{(x - a)^{3/2} (x + a)^{1/2}} = \pm \frac{x - \frac{1}{2} (1 + \sqrt{5})a}{(x - a)^{3/2}} \frac{x - \frac{1}{2} (1 - \sqrt{5})a}{(x + a)^{1/2}}$

$\frac{dy}{dx} > 0$, when $x \in (-\infty, -a] \cup [\frac{1}{2} (1 + \sqrt{5})a, \infty)$

### Example 4: Sketch the curve $y^2 x^2 = x^2 - a^2$

#### Solution
Here,
$y^2 = \frac{x^2 - a^2}{x^2}$

1. Symmetrical about both the axis.
2. It does not pass through origin.
3. $x$-intercepts are $(a, 0)$ and $(-a, 0)$. 
The tangent at $(a, 0)$ is $x = a$ and the tangent at $(-a, 0)$ is $x = -a$.
4. $y = \pm 1$ are the two asymptotes.
5. 
$y = \pm \sqrt{\frac{x^2 - a^2}{x}}$ 
Domain $(-\infty, -a] \cup [a, \infty)$ 
$\frac{dy}{dx} = \pm \frac{a^2}{x^2 \sqrt{x^2 - a^2}}$.

## Hints and Solutions

### INTRODUCTION OF GRAPHS

1. (i) 
The graph consists of two parts: 
- For $x<0$, it is a line segment with equation $y=x-1$. 
- For $x>0$, it is a curve with equation $y=x^2$. 
[The graph has a discontinuity at $x=0$, with a gap between the line and curve segments.]

(ii) 
The graph is a cubic curve with equation $y=x^3$. 
[The graph passes through the origin and has an inflection point at $(0,0)$, with the curve increasing for $x>0$ and decreasing for $x<0$.]

2. (i) 
The graph consists of three parts: 
- For $x<-1$, it is a line segment with equation $y=-2x$. 
- For $-1 \leq x \leq 1$, it is a horizontal line segment with equation $y=2$. 
- For $x>1$, it is a line segment with equation $y=2x$. 

(ii) 
The graph consists of two parts: 
- For $x<4$, it is a line segment with equation $y=x$. 
- For $x \geq 4$, it is a line segment with equation $y=4-x$. 
[The graph has a discontinuity at $x=4$, with a sharp turn from the line $y=x$ to the line $y=4-x$.]

(iii) 
The graph consists of four parts: 
- For $-1 < x < 0$, it is a line segment with equation $y=-x$. 
- For $0 \leq x < 1$, it is a line segment with equation $y=x+1$. 
- For $1 \leq x < 2$, it is a line segment with equation $y=x$. 
- For $2 \leq x < 3$, it is a line segment with equation $y=x+1$. 
- For $x=3$, it is a single point with $y=5$. 
[The graph has discontinuities at $x=0$, $x=1$, and $x=2$, with sharp turns between the line segments.]

(iv) 
The graph consists of three parts: 
- For $-1 < x < 1$, it is a curve with equation $y=x^4$. 
- For $x < -1$ or $x \geq 1$, it is a curve with equation $y=x$. 
[The graph has a discontinuity at $x=-1$ and $x=1$, with a gap between the curve $y=x^4$ and the line $y=x$.]

## Math and Graphs

### Functions and Equations

* $y = \{x - [x]\}$ [A graph of a series of curved lines, each starting at the origin and increasing, then resetting at each integer x-value.]
* $y = (x - [x])^2$ [A graph of a series of parabolic curves, each opening upwards and starting at the origin, then resetting at each integer x-value.]
* $|y| = \{x - [x]\}$ [A graph of a series of curved lines, each starting at the origin and increasing, then resetting at each integer x-value, symmetric about the x-axis.]
* $|y| = (x - [x])^2$ [A graph of a series of parabolic curves, each opening upwards and starting at the origin, then resetting at each integer x-value, symmetric about the x-axis.]

### Linear and Absolute Value Functions

* $y = |x - 1| + |x + 1|$ [A graph of a piecewise linear function, with a minimum at (0, 2) and increasing linearly on either side.]
* $y = -2x$ and $y = 2x$ [Two lines with slopes -2 and 2, intersecting at the origin.]
* $y = [[x - 1]]$ [A graph of a step function, with each step starting at an integer x-value and increasing by 1.]
* $|y| = [[x - 1]]$ [A graph of a step function, with each step starting at an integer x-value and increasing by 1, symmetric about the x-axis.]

## Step 1: Convert the equations to LaTeX format

The equations on the page are:

* $|y| = x + [x]$
* $y = |x| + [[x]]$
* $|y| = x + [x]$
* $|y| = |x| + [[x]]$
* $y = |\sin x|$

These equations can be converted to LaTeX format as follows:

* $|y| = x + [x]$ becomes $|y| = x + \lfloor x \rfloor$
* $y = |x| + [[x]]$ becomes $y = |x| + \lfloor\lfloor x \rfloor\rfloor$
* $|y| = x + [x]$ becomes $|y| = x + \lfloor x \rfloor$
* $|y| = |x| + [[x]]$ becomes $|y| = |x| + \lfloor\lfloor x \rfloor\rfloor$
* $y = |\sin x|$ remains the same.

However, for consistency and accuracy in LaTeX, $\lfloor x \rfloor$ is used to denote the floor function.

#### Section: Play with Graphs

##### Graphs and Equations

The following are descriptions of graphs and their corresponding equations:

*   **[Graph (ii)]:** The graph of $|y| = \frac{1}{2} \sin x$. This graph shows a sinusoidal wave with an amplitude of $\frac{1}{2}$, where the absolute value function reflects the negative portions of the wave to be positive.
*   **[Graph (iii)]:** The graph of $y = |\sin x| + |\cos x|$. This graph represents the sum of the absolute values of sine and cosine functions, creating a waveform with a specific pattern of peaks and troughs.
*   **[Graph (iv)]:** The graph of $|y| = \cos x + |\cos x|$. This graph involves the cosine function and its absolute value, leading to a waveform that reflects certain portions to be positive.
*   **[Graph (v)]:** The graph of $y = \sin^2 x - 2 \sin x$. This equation can be rewritten in terms of $\sin x$ to better understand its form: $y = (\sin x)^2 - 2 \sin x$. Let $u = \sin x$, then $y = u^2 - 2u$. Completing the square gives $y = (u-1)^2 - 1$. Substituting back $u = \sin x$, we get $y = (\sin x - 1)^2 - 1$. This is a parabola in terms of $\sin x$, shifted down by 1.
*   **[Graph (vi)]:** The graph of $y = 2^{\sin x}$. This graph represents an exponential function with a base of 2 and an exponent of $\sin x$, leading to oscillations between $2^{-1}$ and $2^{1}$.

These graphs illustrate various transformations and combinations of basic trigonometric functions and their impact on the resulting waveforms.

### 1. $y=1+x^2-\frac{1}{2}x^4$ 
[The graph of $y=1+x^2-\frac{1}{2}x^4$ is a curve that opens downward with x-intercepts at $(\pm \sqrt{1 + \sqrt{3}},0)$ and $(\pm \sqrt{1 - \sqrt{3}},0)$ and a local maximum at $(0,1)$.]

### 2. $y=(x+1)(x-2)^2$ 
[The graph of $y=(x+1)(x-2)^2$ is a curve that touches the x-axis at $(2,0)$ and crosses the x-axis at $(-1,0)$ with a local minimum at $(2,0)$.]

### 3. $y=\frac{2}{5}x-\frac{1}{2}x^3+\frac{1}{10}x^5$ 
[The graph of $y=\frac{2}{5}x-\frac{1}{2}x^3+\frac{1}{10}x^5$ is a curve with x-intercepts at $(0,0)$ and a wavy shape.]

### 4. $y=\frac{1}{(x+1)(x-1)}$ 
[The graph of $y=\frac{1}{(x+1)(x-1)}$ is a curve with vertical asymptotes at $x=-1$ and $x=1$ and a horizontal asymptote at $y=0$.]

### 5. $y=\frac{x^2-2x-3}{x+1}$ 
[The graph of $y=\frac{x^2-2x-3}{x+1}$ is a curve with a vertical asymptote at $x=-1$ and a slant asymptote at $y=x-3$.]

### 6. $y=\frac{1}{x^2-1}$ 
[The graph of $y=\frac{1}{x^2-1}$ is a curve with vertical asymptotes at $x=-1$ and $x=1$ and a horizontal asymptote at $y=0$.]
